I've finished reading everything and haven't changed any files. Four things need a decision from you before I edit anything: how session logs get produced, auth, where migrations come from, and which setup steps to skip.

## 1. Required runtime and package manager versions

| | Required | This machine |
|---|---|---|
| Node | `>=22.18.0` (`package.json` engines); CI runs Node 22 | v24.11.0 (meets the minimum, but newer than CI) |
| pnpm | `pnpm@10.6.2` (`packageManager`) | 10.6.2 (matches) |
| Postgres | CI uses the `postgres:16` image | Docker 29.6.1 is installed; `psql` isn't, and isn't needed |
| Prisma | 6.19.x | installed by `pnpm install` |

Running `pnpm` prints a warning that the `pnpm.onlyBuiltDependencies` setting is ignored. That setting is what lets Prisma's install scripts run, so I'll check during `pnpm install` whether they actually ran.

## 2. Has template-setup been completed?

**No.** The evidence:
- `package.json` still has `"name": "8x-web-template"`. The skill uses this exact value as its "not yet run" signal.
- `README.md` is still written as template documentation, and `prisma/schema/example.prisma` and the AI spend tracking are still present.
- There's one commit, `6d89899 Initial commit`, and it isn't the template's scaffold message. That means the repo was probably created with GitHub's template feature, so the history-flattening step shouldn't be needed.

## 3. How to verify session logging

**Important: I don't expect this machine to write logs automatically.**
- **There's no separate logging guide in the repo.** The guidance is spread across `CLAUDE.md` ("Session logs"), `docs/rules/secrets.md` ("Session transcripts"), `docs/STANDARDS.md` (the `session-logs-tracked` check), `GEMINI.md`/`QWEN.md`, and step 10 of `.claude/commands/cicd.md`.
- **The logs come from a per-machine hook, not from this repo.** The existing logs' frontmatter says `transcript_source: stdin`, and a past log mentions an extraction script at `~/.codex/extract-log.py`. Your `~/.claude/settings.json` only has Orca hooks and no transcript extractor, so I don't expect anything to appear in `.claude-logs/`.
- **The existing logs were inherited.** All 14 files in `.claude-logs/` were written by the template's authors and contain their usernames, emails and local paths. `.codex-logs/` doesn't exist in git: its only tracked file is `.claude-logs/.gitkeep`.

How to check it's working:
1. After a session, a new `.claude-logs/YYYY-MM-DD_HH-MM-SS_<session-id>.md` should appear, with this session's ID in the frontmatter.
2. `pnpm check:standards` checks that the log folder is set up correctly (tracked, not gitignored, excluded from Prettier). It does **not** check that a log was actually written.
3. Run `pnpm check:secrets` before committing a log (the pre-commit hook runs it too). Also remove any real `DATABASE_URL` from the log yourself first.
4. Stage the log in its own commit, just before `git push`.

If you can't get the org's extraction hook, the fallback is to convert the raw transcript at `~/.claude/projects/C--t1-naisxx/<session-id>.jsonl` into that format.

## 4. Supported local database and migration workflow

- **Database:** Postgres only, set up through Prisma 6. The schema is split across `prisma/schema/*.prisma`. The closest match to CI is `postgres:16` in Docker, with both `DATABASE_URL` and `DIRECT_URL` set to `postgresql://postgres:postgres@localhost:5432/postgres`.
  - The `:6543?pgbouncer=true` pooler address in `.env.example` only applies to hosted Supabase; ignore it locally.
  - `DIRECT_URL` has to be set for every Prisma command except `prisma generate`, because the schema always declares it.
- **Bringing a database up to date:** `pnpm exec prisma migrate deploy`, then `pnpm db:run-once`. There are 7 committed migrations.
- **Changing the schema:**
  1. Edit `.prisma` files only, then run `pnpm prisma:generate`.
  2. Preview what would be generated with `pnpm prisma:migrate:generate:check`.
  3. **CI's `migrations` job writes the migration file** and commits it back to the branch.
  - The only migrations you write by hand are `ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` for each new table, plus data-preserving SQL when the generator refuses a destructive change.
  - One-off data SQL goes in `prisma/run-once.sql`.
  - `pnpm check:rls` can be run against the local database.
- **Problem for a localhost-only trial:** if nobody pushes to GitHub CI, new tables never get a migration, so a fresh clone can't create them. The options are:
  - push to GitHub and let CI generate the file;
  - run the same generator script locally (`scripts/generate-migration.mjs --name … --db-url <local>`), which is CI's tool rather than hand-writing, but the rules reserve it for CI;
  - use `prisma db push` for the local database only, which isn't documented anywhere.

  You need to pick one.

## 5. Conflicts between the README and the detailed rules

1. **Migration command:** README Setup says `pnpm exec prisma migrate dev`. `docs/rules/migrations.md`, the migrations folder's own README and the `dev-onboarding` skill all say to use `migrate deploy` + `db:run-once`, and to use `migrate dev` only with `--create-only` for destructive changes. `migrate dev` can also create migration files. **I'll follow the rules.**
2. **Env file:** the README says to copy to `.env.local`, but the Prisma CLI, `run-once-sql.mjs` and `check-rls.mjs` don't read `.env.local`. Only Next.js does. The README's setup steps will fail unless there's also a `.env` or the variables are exported.
3. **Missing step:** README Setup leaves out `pnpm db:run-once`.
4. **Hosted setup vs. your admin instructions:**
   - README "Deployment", "Before you start" item 5 and the template-setup checklist ask for Vercel, GitHub secrets, `MIGRATIONS_TOKEN`, and Sentry/PostHog/Supabase projects.
   - `.env.example` says `DATABASE_URL` must use the pooler.

   Your instructions override all of this: I'll skip it. Sentry and PostHog do nothing without keys, and the app starts without Supabase settings.
5. **Auth:** `docs/rules/auth.md` defaults to Supabase Auth for any app with user accounts, and a hosted Supabase project isn't required. So either the trial has no accounts (remove auth), or it uses the local Supabase CLI running in Docker. `.gitignore` already expects the local CLI.
6. **Logs:** `GEMINI.md` says "Claude Code writes those transcripts automatically". That's only true with the per-machine hook described in section 3.
7. **Smaller outdated details:**
   - The README says "Twelve topic-scoped rule files"; there are 19.
   - The README's list of rules is incomplete.
   - The README says `pnpm test` only runs `scripts/*.test.mjs`; it also runs `src/**/*.test.mts`.
   - `.gitignore` points to `.claude/rules/migrations.md`, which doesn't exist; the rule is in `docs/rules/migrations.md`.

**Decisions I need from you:**
1. **Logs:** can you get the org's transcript-extraction hook, or should I generate logs from the local `.jsonl` transcripts?
2. **Accounts:** does the trial need user accounts (local Supabase in Docker), or should auth be removed?
3. **New tables:** should their migrations come from GitHub CI, the same generator script run locally, or `db push` for local only?
4. **Template-setup:** OK to run it now, skipping the hosted-infrastructure steps?

Done: no migrations token. Migrations are now generated locally with the repo's own generator, `scripts/generate-migration.mjs`, which is the same one CI runs. I haven't pushed; the change is committed locally on `feat/project-foundation` (`b49adc7`, plus a separate log commit).

**How to add a migration:**
1. Edit `prisma/schema/*.prisma`, then run `pnpm prisma:generate`.
2. Run `pnpm prisma:migrate:generate:check` to preview the SQL. It writes nothing. I ran it against the current schema and it reports nothing pending.
3. Run `pnpm prisma:migrate:generate <name>`. It writes the migration file and applies it to your local database.
4. For each new table, add a small hand-written migration that turns on row-level security, then run `prisma migrate deploy` and `pnpm check:rls`.
5. Commit the schema change and its migrations together.

**Why the generator, not `prisma migrate dev`:** it refuses destructive changes like dropped columns, and its output matches what CI expects. `prisma db push` and `migrate dev` stay off-limits, except `migrate dev --create-only` for hand-written, data-preserving migrations.

**One condition:** the generator compares your local database with the schema. So that database must only ever change through `prisma migrate deploy` or the generator. Any manual change to it would end up in the next migration.

**What CI does now:** the migrations job still runs on every PR, but only as a check (replay, drift, RLS). Because the migration is already committed, there's nothing for it to generate or push, so no token is needed. If a PR ever reached CI with a schema change but no migration, CI would push one itself and that run would wait for manual approval, so always generate it locally first.

**Docs and memory:** I updated `docs/rules/migrations.md`, `AGENTS.md`, the README, and the migrations folder's README so other agents don't follow the old CI-only instructions. I also updated my memory so I won't bring up the token again.

**The gap:**
- **The race:** the hook that runs when you submit a prompt fires before Claude Code writes that prompt to the transcript. The timestamps showed your latest message landing at 14:31:04 and the log being rewritten at 14:31:05 without it.
- **Consequence:** each prompt only reached the log when the turn finished. If a session crashed or was killed mid-turn, that prompt would have been missing.
- **Fix:** the script now takes the prompt directly from the hook's input when it isn't in the transcript yet.

**Other ways a message could have gone missing, also fixed** in `~/.claude/extract-log.py`:
- **Wrong session:** the script logged whichever transcript changed last, so with two sessions open in this repo, one's messages could end up in the other's log. It now uses the transcript the hook names.
- **Prompts with an image:** these were dropped entirely. Their text is now kept, with the image shown as `[image]`.
- **Some slash commands:** a command recorded in a particular format was dropped. Yours happened to be recorded in a format that got through; all user commands are now kept.

A synthetic transcript containing every entry type confirmed each case. Tool output, thinking, interim messages, subagent output, compaction summaries, interrupt markers and background notifications are still excluded. Only the background notifications appear in the transcript as extra "messages", and they're automated events, not yours; the replies to them are still logged.

**Two things to know:**

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=9 session=90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137]
timestamp: 2026-09-30T15:13:42.274Z
time: 2026-09-30 15:13
chars: 8289

I haven't implemented anything or written a plan. I added one concise scope document, linked the product docs from `AGENTS.md` and the README, and reformatted the overview's tables only. Jev isn't documented anywhere in this repo, so nothing below assumes its API, output or score format.

## What PairSight is

**Who it's for:** a business choosing between entity-resolution vendors, for example before a CRM migration. Each vendor claims to find duplicate customer records, but raw match counts can't show who is more accurate. PairSight gives an independent, consistent second opinion on the same records, so the team can see where each vendor over-matches or misses duplicates, with far less manual review.

**What users provide:**
- The original records file, with a unique reference per record, and a mapping of its fields.
- Each vendor's export, as pairs or groups that point back to those references.
- A definition of "same entity": in v1, the same individual, not the same household.

The original file is essential. Without it, PairSight could never find duplicates that a vendor missed.

**How the pieces work together:**
1. **Blocking rules** (shared email, shared phone, surname plus postcode…) group records into overlapping buckets. They only nominate pairs worth checking; they never declare a match.
2. **The comparison set** is every pair from any bucket plus every pair any vendor matched, even pairs no blocking rule would find. Duplicates and reversed pairs count once, and self-pairs are dropped.
3. **Jev** judges each unique pair once, using the record fields and the identity definition. It never sees vendor names, scores or decisions. The same judgment is reused for every vendor.
4. **Our threshold** turns Jev's output into a PairSight decision: match or no match.
5. **The comparator** puts each successfully evaluated pair into one of four categories per vendor: both matched, PairSight only, vendor only, or neither matched. Pending and failed pairs stay visibly incomplete, never treated as nonmatches.

**Our app versus Jev:** the app does everything except judging a single pair: accounts, uploads, validation, mapping, the identity definition, blocking, building the pair set, calling Jev, tracking each pair's status, the threshold, the comparison and pair inspection. Jev does only the pair judgment.

**What results mean, and their limits:**
- The categories measure agreement, not correctness. "Vendor only" could be a vendor error or a PairSight error.
- Everything applies only to the evaluated set. Duplicates hidden in pairs that blocking excluded stay unseen, so a missed duplicate there never shows up.
- Jev is an AI judge and can be wrong, so any precision, recall or F1 figures would be AI estimates for the evaluated set only.

## Worked example

Two vendors, three blocking rules: email, phone, and surname plus postcode.

| Ref | Name | Email | Phone | Postcode | Reality |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Jane Smith | jane.smith@mail | 555-0101 | 10001 | — |
| R2 | Jane Smyth | jane.smith@mail | (blank) | 10001 | same person as R1, surname misspelled |
| R3 | John Smith | john.s@mail | 555-0101 | 10001 | R1's relative: shares her phone and address |
| R4 | Ann Lee | ann@x | 555-0202 | 94103 | — |
| R5 | Anne Lee | a.lee@y | 555-0303 | 94103 | same person as R4 |

**Blocking** produces (R1,R2) from email, (R1,R3) from both phone and Smith+10001, counted once, and (R4,R5) from Lee+94103.

**Vendors:**
- Vendor A exports the group {R1, R2, R3}, which expands to (R1,R2), (R1,R3) and (R2,R3). (R2,R3) isn't in any bucket, but it's added because a vendor matched it.
- Vendor B exports only (R1,R2).

**Comparison set:** 4 unique pairs. Suppose Jev's output, after our threshold, is: (R1,R2) same, (R1,R3) different, (R2,R3) different, and (R4,R5) failed.

| Pair | Vendor A | Vendor B |
|---|---|---|
| R1–R2 | Both matched | Both matched |
| R1–R3 | Vendor only (possible wrong merge of relatives) | Neither matched |
| R2–R3 | Vendor only | Neither matched |
| R4–R5 | Incomplete, no category | Incomplete, no category |

If R4–R5 later succeeds as "same", it becomes **PairSight only** for both vendors: a likely missed duplicate. Pairs like R1–R4 were never evaluated, so they get no category and support no claim.

## Contradictions, gaps and assumptions to check

1. **Metrics versus v1 scope.** The overview's dashboard and score sections describe precision, recall and F1, "uncertain" cases, human correction and exports. The confirmed v1 is only the five steps. The overview itself defers those to planning, so I recorded them as not assumed in v1.
2. **Pair inspection.** The overview's v1 step 5 includes "inspect the underlying records"; your v1 list doesn't mention it.
3. **"Uncertain" versus one threshold.** A single threshold gives only match or no match. "Uncertain" needs a band, or a signal from Jev that we don't know exists.
4. **A vendor's silence may not mean "no match".** Treating an unlisted pair as a vendor nonmatch is only fair if that vendor processed every source record. The overview says vendors "should" have; nothing checks it.
5. **Group expansion is an assumption.** Reading a group of *n* as all n(n−1)/2 pairs assumes the vendor meant every pair, not a chain of links.
6. **The shared comparison set moves.** Adding a vendor adds its pairs, which changes the other vendors' category counts and any recall figure.
7. **"Evaluation configuration" is undefined.** We don't know which changes force Jev to re-evaluate a pair, versus a threshold change, which should only re-decide.
8. **The repo has gaps for this product:**
   - There's no sign-in UI, and nothing calls `provisionUser`.
   - There's no session or ownership guard for ordinary users; the existing guards are admin-only.
   - Supabase Storage is disabled, so where uploads live is undecided.
   - Uploads contain personal data, which must stay out of analytics, logs and Sentry.
9. **"Small dataset" and import formats aren't defined**, and neither are the blocking operators or normalization rules, which matter for phone and email matching.

## Documents updated (uncommitted, for your review)

- **`docs/PairSight-V1-Scope.md` (new):**
  - the confirmed v1 scope
  - the pair and category rules, including "not correctness" and failed or pending never counting as a nonmatch
  - a table of what our app does versus Jev
  - how PairSight fits this repo, including the gaps above
  - 10 open questions

  It links to the overview instead of repeating it.
- **`AGENTS.md`:** a short "Product: PairSight" section linking both documents, with a note not to assume Jev's API. Every agent reads this file.
- **`README.md`:** one intro paragraph with the same two links.
- **`docs/PairSight-Product-Overview.md`:** Prettier alignment only, so the format check passes. I confirmed the wording is unchanged.

All links resolve and the format check passes. The standards check shows only the same 7 known Windows failures. Nothing is committed yet; when you're happy with the docs, I'll commit them with the session log.

## Questions to resolve before planning

1. **Jev:** what is it (an external service, an internal 8x service, or an LLM prompt), and how do we access it locally? What does it take as input and return? Is there a score, what does it mean, and is it deterministic? What are its cost and rate limits? If it calls an AI provider, it goes through the tracked `src/server/ai/` layer.
2. **Threshold:** is it fixed or set by the user? What if Jev returns a label with no score? Is "uncertain" in v1?
3. **Vendor coverage:** must each export cover every source record? What happens to references that aren't in the source file?
4. **Groups:** should a group expand to all its pairs?
5. **Re-evaluation:** which changes trigger it? Is the comparison set frozen per run, or recomputed when a vendor is added?
6. **Blocking in v1:** exact match only, or normalized (phone, email, name)? What bucket size counts as "large"?
7. **Limits:** how many records, vendors and pairs? Is CSV the only format?
8. **Personal data:** may real customer data be sent to Jev? What are the retention and deletion rules?
9. **v1 boundaries:** is pair inspection in v1? Does login come before a first evaluation, or does the demo come first?

<pasted_content id="c33b">
Here are the decisions to use for planning. Treat the numerical limits as initial v1 defaults we can adjust after a real integration test. 
 
1. Jev 
I mean the Jev decision model, accessed through a hosted API from our local application's backend. It is not an internal 8x service or just a prompt to a generic LLM. 
 
Use src/server/ai/ and the existing AI tracking conventions. The input should contain the two records' mapped identity fields and the user's definition of "same individual". Exclude vendor names, 
scores and decisions. 
 
Before finalizing the integration, verify the actual provider, official documentation, credentials, response format, score semantics, pricing, rate limits and data policy. Flag the provider URL/API access as unresolved if those aren't available. Don't assume deterministic outputs or invent a confidence score. 
 
2. Threshold 
If Jev provides a documented same-entity score, use a user-configurable threshold with a clearly explained default. Recommend the default after checking the score semantics and testing fictional examples. 
 
If it returns only a label, use that label and hide the threshold control. Don't manufacture a numeric score. 
 
No separate "uncertain" decision category is required before Friday. Low-confidence cases can be flagged for inspection if the API supports that. Pending and failed evaluations remain separate from all four comparison categories. 
 
3. Vendor coverage 
Every vendor must have evaluated the same source dataset and identity objective for a fair comparison. 
 
A positive-match export does not need to list every source record. For a complete positive-match export, an omitted pair means the vendor did not match it. Explain this assumption during upload and require the user to confirm the export is complete. 
 
Partial exports cannot be treated as complete vendor decisions. For v1, reject that input rather than silently treating unknowns as nonmatches. 
 
Unknown record references are validation errors. Show the affected rows and require correction before evaluation. 
 
4. Groups 
Yes. A vendor group represents all unordered pairs within that group. A group of n records produces n*(n-1)/2 pairs. 
 
Deduplicate member references and resulting pairs. Flag large expansions before running. Never silently truncate vendor matches. 
 
Implement one standard pair CSV format first. Standard group CSV support comes next if time permits before Friday. 
 
5. Re-evaluation 
Freeze the source records, identity definition, blocking configuration, vendor inputs and comparison set for each run. 
 
Changing source data, identity definition or Jev model/request configuration requires a new run and new assessments. 
 
Changing blocking rules changes the candidate set. A new run can reuse successful pair assessments only when the records and judgment configuration are identical. 
 
Changing only the threshold recalculates decisions from saved scores; it does not call Jev again. 
 
Adding a vendor creates a new run/version. Reuse compatible existing assessments and evaluate any newly introduced vendor pairs. Keep completed runs reproducible. 
 
6. Blocking in v1 
Use exact equality on normalized fields, with multiple rules combined using OR. Each rule can use one field or a composite key. 
 
Start with: 
- Email: trim and normalize case; don't strip dots or plus tags. 
- Phone: normalize consistently using explicit country context. 
- Name: trim, normalize case and collapse whitespace. 
- Name plus postcode/location: a composite blocking rule. 
 
Missing values must not create one giant shared bucket. Preserve original values for record inspection. 
 
No fuzzy-name algorithm is required before Friday. Warn when a bucket contains more than 50 records, and show its actual pair expansion. This is a workload warning, not an accuracy boundary. 
 
7. Limits 
Initial before-Friday limits: 
- 500 source records. 
- One vendor required end to end. 
- Up to three vendors if the core flow is stable. 
- 5,000 unique evaluated pairs per run. 
- CSV only. 
 
Validate limits before Jev calls. If the combined candidate set exceeds the limit, explain why and ask the user to reduce the input or tighten blocking. Never silently remove vendor pairs. 
 
Keep these limits configurable. Recommend a lower pair cap if the real Jev cost or rate limits make 5,000 impractical. 
 
8. Personal data 
Use fictional or appropriately anonymized records for the trial and demo. Don't send real identifiable customer data to Jev until the provider's data handling policy and authorization have been established. 
 
Keep application records and results in local Supabase. Don't put uploaded records, pair contents or credentials into agent logs, analytics or AI spend logs. 
 
Plan deletion of an evaluation and its associated uploaded data and results. Avoid retaining temporary upload copies after processing. Don't promise deletion from the external provider without evidence. 
 
9. V1 boundaries 
Yes, pair inspection is essential before Friday. Users need to see the two records, vendor decision, Jev output and PairSight decision. 
 
Require login before creating a real saved evaluation. The fictional demo can be explored before login, using clearly labeled prepared results. Live evaluation runs require login. 
 
Before Friday, prioritize the upload-to-comparison loop, pair inspection, minimum save/reopen behavior and repeatable demo data. 
 
Human corrections, advanced quality metrics, polished exports and a more elaborate landing page come after the first stable loop.
</pasted_content id="c33b">


[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=10 session=90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137]

Your decisions are recorded in `docs/PairSight-V1-Scope.md`, along with what I verified about Jev. Jev turned out to be publicly documented, so most of the Jev unknowns are now settled.

## What I verified about Jev (checked 2026-09-30)

- **Provider:** TypeSafe's hosted "System One" decision model.
- **Two access paths:**
  - TypeSafe's own API: `POST api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`, model `jev-latest`.
  - OpenRouter: `POST openrouter.ai/api/alpha/decisions`, model `typesafe/jev-1.13`. Its responses report the dated version `jev-1.13-20260917` and include the cost.
- **Score format:** a yes/no question type called `noul` returns one value: the probability, from 0 to 1, that the answer is "yes". TypeSafe says it's calibrated across groups of predictions, not guaranteed for any single answer. That's a documented score, so under your rule the threshold is **user-configurable**, and low-confidence cases can be flagged without becoming a category.
- **Threshold guidance:** TypeSafe suggests 0.5 when both kinds of error cost the same, and higher when a false "yes" is costlier. Wrongly merging two people probably is. I haven't picked a default; that waits for the test with fictional pairs.
- **Errors:** 401, 422, 429 and 529, with retry and backoff for the last two.
- **Price:** $0.042 per million input tokens and $0 for output. A 5,000-pair run comes to roughly $0.10, so cost doesn't argue for a lower pair cap. Rate limits still might.
- **Not documented:** rate limits, request size limits, determinism, and API logging or retention.
- **Data policy:** TypeSafe's privacy policy says it doesn't train on input and hosts in the US. It doesn't cover API logging or retention, and I found no data processing agreement, so "fictional data only" stands.

## Issues your decisions surfaced

1. **AI spend tracking doesn't handle Jev yet.** It only prices models whose output is text, and Jev's output type is "decisions". Also, `jev-1.13` isn't in OpenRouter's public model list. As things stand, Jev calls would be logged with no cost and the repo's model check would fail. The tracking code needs a small extension; the table's columns can stay as they are.
2. **Phone normalization with a country context** most likely means adding a library such as `libphonenumber-js`. The repo rules ask for explicit approval of new dependencies.
3. **A response with no score** is recorded as `failed`, never given a made-up score. I added this to the doc; it follows directly from your rules.
4. **No sign-in UI or ordinary-user guard exists yet**, and login is required for saved evaluations. Building that is part of the Friday loop.
5. **The session log records this conversation**, so I'll keep real or uploaded record contents out of my replies, per your personal-data rule.

## Documents updated (not committed yet)

## Still open

Plan how we will deliver PairSight, starting with a working version  ready before Friday, October 2, and then completing the wider app. 
 
Read the product overview, the documents updated during exploration, and the repository's engineering and design instructions. Inspect the actual implementation so the plan builds on what already works. 
 
Do not implement anything yet. 
 
WHAT THE COMPLETE APP IS 
 
PairSight helps users evaluate entity-resolution results. Users upload original customer/person records and matching results from one or more vendors, define what "same entity" means, and use Jev to independently assess candidate record pairs. 
 
The complete experience includes: 
- A landing page explaining the product and a fictional demo. 
- Login and saved evaluations that users can reopen. 
- Source CSV upload, validation, preview and identity-field mapping. 
- Vendor-result upload and mapping of supported pair/group formats. 
- Identity definitions and configurable overlapping blocking rules. 
- A comparison-plan preview showing candidate counts and coverage. 
- Jev evaluation with progress, visible failures and retry handling. 
- A vendor comparison dashboard and side-by-side record inspection. 
- Human review recorded separately from AI judgments. 
- Clearly qualified quality metrics where coverage supports them. 
- CSV findings and summary exports. 
 
The app runs locally with the existing Supabase Auth and Prisma setup. Keep the initial domain focused on customer/person records. Broader entity types and large-scale processing are later extensions. 
 
VERSION READY BEFORE FRIDAY 
 
Prioritize this complete, demonstrable loop: 
 
1. Upload source records and vendor results. Map unique record references and identity fields. Validate missing/duplicate references and unknown vendor references. 
2. Define identity and blocking rules. Specify what counts as the same individual. Create overlapping candidate buckets using a small, useful set of supported blocking operators. 
3. Build the comparison set. Combine blocked candidates with ALL vendor-matched pairs. Deduplicate across buckets and vendors, collapse reversed pairs, and exclude self-pairs. Show counts before evaluation and flag oversized expansions. 
4. Evaluate with real Jev integration. Assess each unique pair using its records and identity definition. 
Apply our configured threshold to obtain system match decisions. Show progress and keep failed/pending assessments incomplete. 
5. Compare the two sets for each uploaded vendor: 
   - Both matched: vendor and PairSight agree it is a match. 
   - PairSight only: potential duplicate the vendor missed. 
   - Vendor only: potential incorrect vendor match OR a PairSight error. 
   - Neither matched: both classify an evaluated pair as different entities. 
Show category counts, useful filters, and side-by-side records so someone can inspect an actual disagreement. 
 
Support one vendor end to end first; add multiple vendors before Friday only if the core loop is stable. Preserve a structure that supports multiple vendors without duplicating evaluation work. 
 
Use the existing authentication. Include the minimum persistence needed to retain and reopen the demo evaluation. Prioritize a small fictional dataset and vendor export that demonstrate meaningful disagreements. Clearly distinguish prepared demo results from live Jev evaluations. 
 
PRIORITY AND SCOPE 
 
Treat the before-Friday version as a usable product slice, not a set of disconnected screens. 
Prioritize the upload-to-results flow over landing-page polish, advanced metrics, human correction workflows, elaborate exports, vendor-specific adapters and additional blocking algorithms. 
 
For every wider-app feature, state whether it is: 
- Required before Friday. 
- Next after the first working demo. 
- Deferred until the core product is complete. 
 
Make the earliest milestone a running vertical slice so we can test the integration risks immediately. Avoid scheduling all backend work first and leaving integration or usable UI until the end. 
 
TECHNICAL AND PRODUCT DECISIONS 
 
Verify Jev's actual API, authentication, inputs, outputs, score meaning, limits and failure behavior from available authoritative documentation. Identify what evidence or access is missing rather than guessing. Make a small real integration check an early milestone. 
 
Determine practical v1 choices for: 
- Supported CSV and vendor-result formats. 
- Pair versus group imports and group-to-pair expansion. 
- Blocking operators, normalization and missing-value handling. 
- Dataset and candidate-pair limits. 
- Identity-definition input and threshold behavior. 
- Evaluation execution, progress, persistence and retries. 
- Ownership checks and isolation between users. 
- Reuse of the existing API, database, auth and UI conventions. 
 
Explain the important tradeoffs and recommend simple defaults. Use DRY where logic genuinely repeats and avoid premature abstractions. 
 
Vendor identity, scores and match decisions must not influence Jev's pair judgment. Reuse each pair assessment across vendors within the same evaluation configuration. 
 
Agreement categories are not ground truth. Neither matched covers only evaluated pairs. Do not turn failed assessments into nonmatches. Do not claim dataset-wide recall/F1 from blocked candidate coverage. Keep identity rules, blocking settings, Jev configuration and threshold associated with the results they produced. 
 
UI AND VERIFICATION 
Plan the actual user journey and screens, including loading, empty, validation-error, failure, retry and success states. 
 
Use DESIGN.md and existing components. Identify where visual references are needed and what qualities they should guide. Prioritize clear field mapping, understandable blocking configuration and readable comparisons. 
 
Include meaningful automated checks and manual verification of the complete flow. Cover candidate deduplication, inclusion of vendor pairs outside blocking, threshold decisions, comparison categories, invalid references and evaluation failures. 
 
Use local Prisma migrations and retain session logs with related work. Organize delivery into coherent feature branches and reviewed milestones. Run checks locally before pushing and avoid unnecessary CI runs. 
 
WHAT I WANT FROM THE PLAN 
 
1. Your understanding of the complete app and the before-Friday version. 
2. A realistic sequence from now until the demo is ready, with concrete outcomes and dependencies rather than unsupported hour estimates. 
3. Recommended architecture and data models using the existing stack. 
4. The screens and end-to-end user flow. 
5. Feature milestones, suggested branch boundaries and acceptance criteria. 
6. The critical risks to verify early, especially Jev and import formats. 
7. A repeatable demo scenario showing the four comparison categories. 
8. The roadmap from the Friday version to the complete app. 
9. Any decisions you genuinely need from me before implementation. 
 
Be decisive about routine choices. Ask only questions that materially change scope or architecture. Keep the plan practical enough to execute. 
 
Once we agree on the plan, we will save it into the repository as the implementation reference before starting feature development.

I have what I need from the code. The plan below builds on what's already in place, and I haven't implemented anything. It uses the existing Supabase auth helpers, Prisma with local migrations and RLS, the server-action and route factories, the AI tracking layer, TanStack Query, next-intl, and the small `ui/` component set.

Research sources for Jev are the ones cited in `docs/PairSight-V1-Scope.md`.

# PairSight delivery plan

## 1. What we're building

**The complete app** lets a signed-in user create saved evaluations:
- upload source records and one or more vendor exports, and map their fields
- define "same individual" and overlapping blocking rules
- preview the comparison plan
- run Jev on every unique pair, with progress and retries
- compare each vendor against PairSight's decisions in four agreement categories, and inspect any pair side by side
- later: record human review separately, see qualified quality metrics, and export findings

A public demo shows prepared results on fictional data.

**The Friday version (2026-10-02)** is one usable slice, not separate screens:

> sign in → create evaluation → upload source CSV and one vendor pair CSV → map fields and fix validation errors → define identity and blocking → review the plan → run real Jev with visible progress and failures → compare in four categories with filters → inspect a disagreement → reopen it later

Plus a public, clearly labeled demo. Multiple vendors come only if the core loop is stable. The data model supports several vendors from day one, so adding them never duplicates Jev work.

## 2. Architecture

**Layers**, reusing existing conventions:

| Layer | Location | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| Pure domain logic (unit-tested, no I/O) | `src/server/pairsight/` | `csv.ts` (parse and validate), `normalize.ts`, `blocking.ts`, `pairs.ts` (canonical order, dedupe, group expansion, union), `limits.ts`, `decisions.ts` (threshold and categories), `judgment.ts` (Jev request builder, response parser, config key) |
| Data access, always owner-scoped | `src/server/pairsight/store.ts` | Every function takes `ownerId`. Another user's evaluation returns 404, not 403. |
| Run engine | `src/server/pairsight/run-engine.ts` | Claims pending assessments and calls Jev (details below) |
| Jev provider | `src/server/ai/typesafe.ts` | The only file that calls TypeSafe; wrapped in `trackAiCall` like `anthropic.ts` |
| Auth | `src/server/auth/session.ts` | `requireUser()`: Supabase session, then `provisionUser`, then the `User` row |
| Mutations | server actions via `validatedAction` | Zod input, `AppError` for user-caused failures |

**AI tracking:**
- Add provider `typesafe` with a usage extractor for `{input_tokens, output_tokens}`.
- Record feature `pairsight_pair_judgment`, with `meta` holding ids only.
- The pricing gap from the scope doc (TypeSafe's output type isn't in the rate card) gets resolved in Milestone 1.

**How runs execute: a client-driven loop over work claimed in the database.** Starting a run freezes a snapshot and creates the pairs and pending assessments. The run page then repeatedly calls `advanceRun`, which:
- claims a batch of 10 pending assessments, plus any "running" ones older than 60 seconds (reclaimed after a crash or hot reload)
- calls Jev with concurrency 4 and a 20-second timeout
- retries 429, 529, timeouts and network errors up to 3 times with backoff
- marks 422 and unparseable responses as `failed` straight away
- treats 401 as a configuration error that stops the whole run
- returns progress counts

Closing the page pauses the run; reopening resumes it. "Retry failed" resets failed pairs to pending.

*Tradeoff:* a background worker would keep running without the page open, but `after()` only lasts the route's maximum duration, and a queue is infrastructure we don't need on localhost. The loop is simple, resumable and easy to test. A worker is a later extension.

**Data model** (new file `prisma/schema/pairsight.prisma`; every table gets an RLS migration):

| Model | Key fields |
|---|---|
| `Evaluation` | `ownerId?` (null for the demo), `name`, `kind` (live or demo); draft configuration: `identitySame`, `identityDifferent`, `countryCode`, `jevFields[]`, `blockingRules` (JSON), `threshold` |
| `SourceDataset` | `evaluationId`, `fileName`, `rowCount`, `referenceColumn`, `columnRoles` (JSON). Immutable: replacing the file creates a new dataset. |
| `SourceRecord` | `datasetId`, `reference` (unique per dataset), `rowNumber`, `values` (JSON, original values) |
| `VendorExport` | `evaluationId`, `datasetId`, `name`, `format` (pair, later group), `mapping`, `confirmedCompleteAt`, `pairCount` |
| `VendorMatch` | `exportId`, `recordAId`, `recordBId` (canonical order, unique) |
| `Run` | `evaluationId`, `datasetId`, `number`, `origin` (live or prepared), `status`, plus a **frozen snapshot**: the identity definition, `jevFields`, country, blocking rules, `exportIds[]`, `judgmentKey`, requested model, `threshold` |
| `RunPair` | `runId`, `recordAId`, `recordBId`, `ruleIds[]` (which rules produced it), `matchedByExportIds[]`, `assessmentId` |
| `Assessment` | `datasetId`, record A/B, `judgmentKey`, `status` (pending, running, succeeded or failed), `score?`, `modelReported`, `requestId`, `attempts`, `errorCode`, `claimedAt`, `completedAt`, `origin` |
| *Later:* `HumanReview` | `runPairId`, `reviewerId`, `verdict` (same, different or unsure), `note`. Kept separate from AI judgments. |

- **Reuse:** `judgmentKey` is a hash of the identity definition, `jevFields`, country, requested model and request-template version. The threshold is not part of it. Assessments are unique per (dataset, pair, key), so a new run with new vendors or blocking rules reuses succeeded assessments and only sends new pairs to Jev.
- **Categories are computed when read**, from the run's threshold and `matchedByExportIds`, so a threshold change never calls Jev. The threshold stays on the run and is shown wherever results appear.

## 3. v1 choices

| Topic | Choice |
|---|---|
| **Source CSV** | UTF-8, BOM allowed, header row, comma-delimited, ≤ 1 MB, ≤ 500 rows. Empty cell = missing. Trimmed reference, required and unique; errors list the row numbers. |
| **Vendor formats** | Standard pair CSV first: the user picks the "record A" and "record B" columns, and other columns (vendor scores) are ignored and never sent to Jev. Self-pair rows are skipped with a count, and duplicate or reversed rows collapse. Standard group CSV (`record_ref`, `group_id`) comes next: a group of n records expands to n(n−1)/2 pairs, members are de-duplicated, and a large expansion is flagged. |
| **Vendor validation** | An unknown reference is an error listing the affected rows and blocks the run. The user must confirm the export is complete, with the omitted-pair assumption explained. |
| **Field roles** | full name, first name, last name, email, phone, date of birth, address, city, postcode, country, other. Jev receives the mapped fields the user selects (default: all), with original values. |
| **Blocking** | Rules = one field or a composite (AND); rules combine with OR; exact equality on normalized values. Email: trim and lowercase. Phone: E.164 using the evaluation's country. Name: trim, lowercase, collapse spaces. Postcode: trim, uppercase, remove spaces. A missing or unparseable value gets no key. Presets: Email, Phone, Name, Name + postcode. |
| **Limits** (in `limits.ts`) | 500 records, 3 vendors, 5,000 unique pairs, a warning above 50 records per bucket (showing its pair count), 1 MB files. Checked before any Jev call; an over-limit plan is blocked with a per-rule and per-vendor breakdown; vendor pairs are never dropped. |
| **Identity definition** | Two text areas, "Counts as the same individual" and "Does not count", prefilled with editable defaults. They map directly onto Jev's `criteria.true` and `criteria.false`. The request's `state` holds only the two records' fields and the country: no references, vendor names or scores. |
| **Threshold** | A decision is a match when `noul ≥ threshold`. Users can change it on the results page, with an explanation. The default comes from the Milestone 1 test (0.5 until then). Scores within a band around the threshold get a "low confidence" flag, a filter rather than a category. |
| **Sign-in** | Email and password against local Supabase (confirmations are off locally), handled in server actions through the existing auth-only client. Live evaluations require login. |
| **Ownership** | `requireUser()` in every action and page; all queries are scoped by `ownerId`; the demo evaluation is read-only through a dedicated reader, and mutations reject it. Tested with two users. |
| **UI conventions** | Tokens and existing `ui/` components; add Select, Checkbox, Progress and Tabs only when a screen uses them; next-intl strings; filters in URL params; TanStack Query for the run loop. |

## 4. Screens and states

| Screen | Contents | States |
|---|---|---|
| `/` landing (minimal) | The problem, three steps, **Try demo**, **Start evaluation** | — |
| `/sign-in`, `/sign-up` | Email and password | Invalid credentials, success redirect |
| `/evaluations` | Saved evaluations: name, status, last run | Empty state with create and demo actions |
| `/evaluations/[id]` workspace | Stepper: Records → Vendors → Rules → Plan → Run → Results, each showing complete or needs attention | — |
| Records | Upload, preview of the first 10 rows, reference column plus a role per column | Parsing; file errors (size, encoding, header); missing or duplicate reference table; success summary |
| Vendors | Name, file, A/B column selection, required completeness checkbox, vendor list | Unknown-reference table; self-pair and duplicate counts; success |
| Rules | Identity text areas, country, fields sent to Jev, preset or custom rules with live counts per rule (buckets, pairs, largest bucket) | Warning above 50 records per bucket; no-rules state |
| Plan | Records; blocked, vendor, outside-blocking and unique pairs; reduction versus all pairs; reused assessments; limit check; **Start evaluation** | Over-limit block with its cause; unresolved validation errors block starting |
| Run | Progress bar; succeeded, failed and pending counts; note that the run pauses if the page is closed | Running, paused/resume, config-error banner (e.g. invalid key), failures with **Retry failed**, complete |
| Results | Vendor tabs (when there's more than one); four category counts plus an incomplete count; threshold control; "agreement, not correctness" wording; filters (category, outside blocking, low confidence, failed or pending); pairs table | Empty filter result; incomplete-run warning |
| Pair inspection | Both records side by side, original values, differences highlighted, each vendor's decision, Jev score/model/time, decision at the current threshold, rules that produced the pair (or "added by vendor") | Pending or failed shown explicitly, never as "no match" |
| `/demo` | The results and inspection screens, read-only, with a banner: "Prepared results recorded from Jev \<model\> on \<date\> — fictional data" | — |

**Visual references wanted** for the field-mapping table, the rules builder, the category summary with the pairs table, and the side-by-side diff. They should aim for dense but readable tables and a calm, neutral look.

Categories must **not** use success or danger colors. They mean agreement, not right or wrong, and `DESIGN.md` reserves status colors for status.

## 5. Milestones and branches

Order: M0 → M1 → M2 → M3 → M4. M2 and M3 depend on M1's schema and Jev client, and M4's prepared results depend on M3. Each milestone passes `local-review` before its push, and each branch commits its schema, migrations and session logs together.

| Branch | What it delivers | Acceptance |
|---|---|---|
| **M0 `docs/product-plan`** | This plan saved as `docs/PairSight-Delivery-Plan.md`, linked from `AGENTS.md`, together with the scope docs | Docs merged |
| **M1 `feat/jev-vertical-slice`** (retires the biggest risks first) | 1. `typesafe.ts`, the tracking extension, and a `pnpm jev:check` live script on fictional pairs (it prints shape, latency and model, never record content). 2. The complete Prisma schema, migrations and RLS. 3. Sign-in/up and `requireUser`. 4. A rough but real flow: create evaluation → upload source and vendor CSVs with basic mapping → preset email rule → run with real Jev → a basic category table | A live call returns a documented `noul` and gets a tracking row. Signed in, the demo CSVs run end to end and show all four categories. A second user gets 404. We've recorded whether a pinned model is accepted, whether repeated calls agree, whether we hit 429s, and evidence for the default threshold. |
| **M2 `feat/evaluation-setup`** | Full mapping UI, every validation state, completeness confirmation, rules builder with normalization, plan preview, limits and bucket warnings | Invalid references, duplicates and over-limit plans are blocked with clear errors; counts are correct; vendor pairs outside blocking appear in the plan |
| **M3 `feat/run-and-results`** | Engine hardening (retries, stale reclaim, config error, retry failed, resume), reuse across runs, threshold control, dashboard, filters, inspection | A forced failure stays incomplete, retry succeeds, a threshold change recomputes without calling Jev, a disagreement can be inspected |
| **M4 `feat/demo`** | Final fictional dataset, prepared snapshot, idempotent `pnpm demo:seed`, public `/demo`, minimal landing, e2e with a Jev stub, manual checklist | Friday readiness gate: `local-review`, e2e and the manual flow all pass |
| *Optional:* multiple vendors | Up to 3 vendors, vendor tabs, shared assessments | Only once the gate above is green |

**Cut line if time runs short:** drop multiple vendors first, then the landing page (use a bare link page), then the plan's reduction statistic. The loop, inspection, save/reopen and the demo stay.

## 6. Testing

**Unit tests** (`node --test`, pure domain code):
- **CSV:** BOM, quoted commas and newlines, missing or duplicate references, row and size limits
- **Normalization:** email, phone with country, name, postcode
- **Blocking:** overlapping buckets; missing values never share a bucket; bucket-size warnings
- **Pairs:** canonical order, reversed pairs collapse, self-pairs excluded, dedupe across buckets and vendors, **vendor pairs outside blocking included**, group expansion n(n−1)/2 with member dedupe
- **Limits:** over-cap explanations; vendor pairs never dropped
- **Decisions:** threshold boundary at `≥`, all four categories, **failed and pending never categorized**
- **Judgment key:** a threshold change doesn't change the key; the other inputs do
- **Jev parsing:** a valid `noul`; a missing or out-of-range value becomes `failed`
- **Retries:** 429 and 529 retry; 401 is a config error; 422 fails

**E2E (Playwright):**
- Uses a local Jev stub via a `TYPESAFE_BASE_URL` override, so no key is needed in CI. The stub returns scripted scores, one 529-then-success and one permanent failure.
- The flow: sign up → upload → rules → plan counts → run → category counts → inspection → retry failed; plus the public demo; plus an ownership check with two users.

**Manual:** the full live flow with the real key on fictional data, and a check that no record content appears in logs, analytics or `ai_usage`.

## 7. Demo scenario

About 24 fictional people, Vendor A as a pair CSV, and rules Email, Phone, and Last name + postcode. Designed cases:

| Case | Designed so that | Expected category |
|---|---|---|
| Same person, email case and phone format differ | Vendor matches it | Both matched |
| Misspelled surname, same email and date of birth | Vendor misses it | **PairSight only** |
| Relatives sharing a phone and address, different first names and birth dates | Vendor matches them | **Vendor only** |
| Same common name and postcode, different birth date and email | Vendor doesn't match | **Neither matched** |
| Same person who moved (new email, phone and postcode) | Vendor matches; no rule catches it | Both matched, **outside blocking** |
| Two unrelated people | Vendor matches | Vendor only, outside blocking |
| Nickname with a missing birth date | Borderline | Low-confidence flag |
| Records with blank email or phone | — | Shows that blanks don't form a giant bucket |

These are designed expectations, not results. We run real Jev on the dataset in M1 and M4, adjust the fixtures until every category is populated, and store that real run as the prepared snapshot, with its model id and date. No scores are made up. Failure states are demonstrated through the stubbed e2e test, not the demo data. An optional Vendor B (cautious) shows the multi-vendor view.

## 8. Wider-app roadmap

| Feature | When |
|---|---|
| Login and saved evaluations (list and reopen) | Before Friday |
| Source upload, validation, preview, mapping | Before Friday |
| Vendor pair CSV with completeness confirmation | Before Friday |
| Identity definition and blocking rules | Before Friday |
| Comparison-plan preview and limits | Before Friday |
| Jev evaluation with progress, failures and retry | Before Friday |
| Four-category comparison, filters, inspection | Before Friday |
| Prepared fictional demo (public) | Before Friday |
| Minimal landing page | Before Friday (bare version) |
| Multiple vendors (up to 3) | Before Friday if stable, otherwise next |
| Standard group CSV | Next |
| Delete an evaluation with all its data | Next (early; required before any real data) |
| Human review, separate from AI judgments | Next |
| CSV findings export | Next |
| Qualified metrics (precision on evaluated vendor pairs; recall and F1 labeled "evaluated set only") | Next |
| Summary export, polished landing, analytics events | Next |
| Background worker (runs without the page open) | Deferred |
| Fuzzy or phonetic blocking, more operators | Deferred |
| Vendor-specific adapters, XLSX | Deferred |
| Sampling excluded pairs, verified ground-truth labels | Deferred |
| Companies and products, larger scale | Deferred |
| Real customer data | Deferred until TypeSafe's data handling and our authorization are established |

## 9. Risks to check early (M1 unless noted)

1. **Jev access and behavior:** the key works; the response matches the docs; a pinned model is or isn't accepted; latency; 429s under concurrency 4; whether repeated calls agree.
2. **Jev's judgment on our wording:** does it separate relatives sharing a household? Choose the default threshold from real scores on fictional pairs.
3. **AI tracking fit:** pricing and `check:ai-models`, resolved with the smallest change and no column changes.
4. **CSV variety:** Excel exports (BOM, quotes, CRLF). A strict v1 with clear errors.
5. **Pair explosion:** counts before every run, and caps. Groups come later.
6. **Dev-server restarts mid-run:** handled by reclaiming stale claims (M3).
7. **Data leaks between users:** owner-scoped data access and a two-user e2e test.
8. **Friday timing:** protected by the cut line and M1 being a real end-to-end slice.

## Decisions I need from you

1. **Two new dependencies:** `papaparse` for CSV parsing (quoting, embedded newlines and BOM are easy to get wrong by hand) and `libphonenumber-js` for phone normalization with a country. Alternatives: a hand-written parser, and digits-only phone matching with no country. May I add both?
2. **Jev cost in `ai_usage`:** TypeSafe doesn't publish its own price. Should Jev rows record a NULL cost (my recommendation: honest, and counted as "unpriced" on the dashboard), or use OpenRouter's $0.042 per million tokens as a stand-in?

Other things you'll need to do:
- **Before M1's live check:** add the TypeSafe key to `.env.local` yourself. I'll name the variable in `.env.example` and `src/lib/env.ts`.
- **When you agree the plan:** I'll save it as `docs/PairSight-Delivery-Plan.md`, link it from `AGENTS.md`, and commit it with the scope docs and session log on `docs/product-plan`.

Use this brand palette:
- Primary: #E45742
- Supporting: #972828, #EB7F31, #FCAD38
Plan complete light and dark themes using semantic design tokens for backgrounds, surfaces, text, borders, actions, and statuses. Use neutral surfaces to keep data readable, with restrained brand accents. Verify accessible contrast in both themes, and keep brand colors distinct from error, warning, and success meanings. Support light, dark, and system preferences.
Before proposing screens, map the user journey from new evaluation through source/vendor uploads, column mapping, validation, blocking configuration, evaluation, results comparison, pair inspection, and export. Follow the agreed product behavior and flag unresolved decisions rather than inventing functionality.

1. Navigation and screen hierarchy, with one obvious primary action per stage.

3. Readable results tables, useful filters, and side-by-side pair inspection. Clearly distinguish vendor disagreement, evaluation outcomes, and pairs excluded by blocking.

5. Consistent typography, spacing, components, responsive behavior, keyboard access, and subtle interaction feedback.

The approved UI/UX plan is saved in the repo as `docs/PairSight-UX-Plan.md` and committed on `docs/product-plan`. As you asked, nothing is implemented yet: no tokens, components or screens.

**Your two decisions are recorded:**
- `next-themes` handles Light / Dark / System switching.
- A text wordmark ("PairSight" plus a small brand-colored pair mark) stands in until a real logo exists.

**Main design choices:**
- **Brand contrast:** white text on `#E45742` only reaches 3.66:1, below the 4.5:1 needed for normal text. So light mode fills primary buttons with a darker shade of the same hue, `#C9432F` (4.85:1). Dark mode uses `#E45742` itself, with dark text on it (5.02:1).
- **Orange and amber** (`#EB7F31`, `#FCAD38`) appear only in the wordmark and on the landing page. Keeping them out of the product UI stops them being confused with warnings.
- **Errors** use a crimson (hue 345°) instead of the brand's red-orange (8°), and always come with an icon and text.
- **Contrast:** every proposed color pair measures at least 4.5:1 for text and 3:1 for input borders and chart marks, in both themes. Faint text was the one that needed changing: the old gray measured 3.92:1.
- **Comparison categories:** blue, violet, teal and gray, deliberately not the brand or status colors, so they never read as right or wrong. The two disagreement categories (PairSight only, Vendor only) are the visually strong ones.
- **Design order:** the Results and Pair detail screens are built first, against demo fixture data, and the rest of the app follows their visual system.
- **Review gate before Friday:** screenshots of every screen and state in both themes at three widths, an accessibility audit, a keyboard-only pass, and a walk through the journey as a new user.

**Changes made:**
- `docs/PairSight-UX-Plan.md` is new, and `AGENTS.md` links it.
- The delivery plan's approved decisions now include shadcn/ui with Phosphor icons, shadcn charts, and `next-themes`.
- `DESIGN.md` gets the new tokens when M1 implements them.
- The docs commit is `080ab24`, with the session log committed separately in `c90d87d`.

**Taken from your system:**

- **An input-border token** (`#8C7B71` light, `#857469` dark). Both of our regular borders fail as a form-control edge (1.3–1.8:1, needs 3:1). These pass on every surface they sit on.

**Adjusted:**

**What works now:**
- **The full flow:** sign up or sign in, create an evaluation, upload records, add a vendor, set rules, review the plan, run, then Results and Pair detail, with filters, J/K/Esc navigation, and the step rail showing where you are.
- **Rules you set:** failed or pending pairs are shown as incomplete, never as "no match". Changing the threshold re-decides without calling Jev. A second run reuses all 8 earlier judgments.
- **Failure handling:** 429 and 529 responses are retried; a permanent failure stays incomplete after a retry; a missing or rejected key stops the run with instructions and can be resumed.
- **Design:** the merged light and dark palette, with a test that checks every color pairing's contrast; `next-themes`; and shadcn components using Phosphor icons.

## What `/ai-usage-check` does, and whether it's useful
It checks two things: that AI calls still work, and that each one is still being costed in `ai_usage`. The second matters more, because when costing breaks, calls keep working and the spend report just quietly undercounts. It worked in three layers:

1. **Static checks:** `check:ai-models` found all 8 mapped models still priced upstream. Typecheck and unit tests passed.
2. **One real call, read back from the database:** I made a single real Jev call through the app's own tracked code path, using a fictional pair and a separate feature tag, `pairsight_ai_usage_check`. The row came back correct:
   - model stored as `typesafe/jev-1.13`
   - 472 input and 23 output tokens
   - priced at 20 micro-USD (about $0.00002, as expected)
   - no prompt or record content saved
3. **Test coverage:** this is where it found a real gap. The new Jev usage reader had no unit test, so I added one to `src/server/lib/ai-usage/ai-usage.test.mts`.

It also turned up 30 unpriced rows from yesterday's `jev:check`, logged before the model ID was fixed to `jev-1.13.0`. They show the safety net working: an unknown model is stored as unpriced rather than as $0.

**Is it useful?** Yes, whenever a change touches a model, the pricing, or a new AI call. It's the only check that proves a real row gets written and priced, and in our case it caught a missing test. For branches that don't touch AI, it's overkill.

`jev:check` writing to `ai_usage` directly doesn't break anything. Its rows use their own feature tag and are priced the same way the app's are.

## Blocking strategy

It's multi-pass blocking with overlapping buckets (`src/server/pairsight/blocking.ts`):

- **How it works:** each rule is an exact match on a normalized key, which is either one field or a composite of several. A record joins one bucket per rule, and every pair of records within a bucket becomes a candidate.
- **Rules combine with OR:** a pair found by several rules is kept once and remembers which rules found it. That's where "Found by rules: Phone; Name + postcode" on the pair page comes from.
- **Missing values:** a record with no usable value for a rule simply isn't in that rule's buckets, so blanks never match each other.
- **Default rules** on a new evaluation are **email**, **phone** and **name + postcode**. The Rules screen also offers **name** alone and **last name + postcode**.
- **Normalization** only affects blocking keys. Jev always receives the original values.

| Field | How it's normalized |
|---|---|
| Email | Trimmed and lowercased. Dots and +tags are kept, so `jane+x@` and `jane@` land in different buckets. |
| Phone | Parsed with libphonenumber to E.164 using the evaluation's country; impossible numbers are dropped |
| Name | Uses full name if mapped, otherwise first + last. Unicode-normalized, lowercased, spaces collapsed. |
| Postcode | Uppercased with spaces removed |

**Which pairs Jev compares:** the blocked pairs plus every pair a vendor matched, even when no rule would have found it. The UI labels those as "Added by vendor", and they're how "Vendor only" disagreements get found.

**Limits:**
- The whole comparison set is capped at `LIMITS.maxPairsPerRun`.
- Any rule with a bucket above 50 records gets a warning.
- Blocking only proposes candidates; Jev plus the threshold make the decision.

**Cleanup item:** `blocking.ts` also exports an unused `PRESET_RULES` list of 4 rules. It duplicates `RULE_PRESETS` in `rules-preview.ts`, which has 5 (it adds last name + postcode). It's dead code that has already drifted, so I'll delete it early in M2.

## M2 (`feat/evaluation-setup`)

The delivery plan scoped M1 as a rough flow with basic mapping and an email-only rule. We went further, so most of what M2 lists is already built:

| M2 item | Status |
|---|---|
| Column-mapping UI with suggested roles | Done |
| Validation: missing or duplicate references, unknown vendor references, CSV and limit errors, row numbers with "and N more" | Done |
| Vendor completeness confirmation | Done |
| Rules builder: preset toggles with live bucket and pair stats and the bucket-size warning | Done |
| Plan preview: pairs from rules, pairs added by vendors, overlap, the cap, reused assessments, cost estimate | Done |
| e2e for invalid references and for vendor pairs outside blocking reaching the plan | Done |

**What's left in M2:**
1. **Custom composite rule:** the "Advanced" option on the Rules screen to build a 1–3 field rule. The backend already accepts saved custom rules, but there's no UI for it yet.
2. **Over-cap plan:** the plan currently just says "too many pairs". The UX plan asks it to name the rule or vendor causing the overflow and suggest fixes.
3. **Test gaps the acceptance criteria call for:** an e2e for an over-limit plan being blocked, a duplicate-reference case, and unit tests that pin the plan counts.
4. **Planned setup polish not built yet:**
   - autosaving draft config
   - a 10-row preview on Records
   - a step picker instead of the side rail on narrow screens
   - the normalization summary line on each rule card, if it's missing
5. **The cleanup above,** plus the refinements you've been noting.

So M2 is mostly gaps and hardening rather than new screens. Once you share your refinement notes, I can fold them in and propose a scope.

When someone changes the country for phone numbers on the Rules screen, a small warning now appears above the rule cards. It says the counts below still read phone numbers as the saved country (e.g. US), and has a **Save and recount** button. Nothing is committed yet. All 8 e2e specs pass, including a new one for this, and so do the build, lint, typecheck and format checks.

- **When it shows:** only while the typed country differs from the saved one, ignoring case, so retyping `us` hides it again. It also only shows if a phone column is mapped, since phone parsing is the only thing the country affects.
- **Save and recount:** saves your current settings and stays on the page, so the preset counts reload with the new country. The other button, **Review plan**, saves and moves on as before. If you'd rather have the warning without the extra button, it's one line to remove.
- **Accessibility:** the notice uses a polite status role, so screen readers aren't interrupted on every keystroke.

The new e2e spec checks that:
- typing GB shows the notice and typing back to US hides it
- **Save and recount** stays on the Rules page and changes the phone counts (the demo's US numbers don't read as British)
- GB is still there after a reload

The changes are in `rules/unsaved-country-notice.tsx` (new), `rules-form.tsx`, two new messages in `messages/en.json`, and the new spec in `e2e/pairsight.spec.ts`.

**Test tooling problem, twice now:** a local e2e run can leave its dev server running on port 3000 afterwards. Both times it came from a run that had just failed. The next run reuses that server, and the dev cache in `.next/dev/cache` kept serving the old `en.json`, so new text showed up as raw keys like `Rules.countryUnsaved`. Stopping the server and deleting that cache folder fixes it. It's worth a small safeguard later, such as a script that clears that cache before local e2e runs.

The overlay is on top at the header too, so clicks there are blocked. The lighter look in the screenshot is just how Chromium renders the header's frosted-glass blur under an overlay. Not a bug.

The Rules screen now tracks unsaved changes and asks before you leave. Nothing is committed yet. Lint, typecheck, format, env, secrets, the build and 9 of 9 e2e specs pass; the only unit-test failures are the same 10 Windows-only ones.

**What counts as unsaved:** the form compares what "Review plan" would save against what's saved. That covers the two identity definitions, the country, the fields sent to Jev, which rules are on, and added or removed custom rules. Undoing an edit makes the form clean again. Saving is never treated as leaving.

**How each way of leaving is handled:**

| Exit | What happens |
|---|---|
| Any in-app link (header, side steps, breadcrumb) | Our dialog: **Leave without saving?** with **Stay on this page** and **Leave without saving**. "Stay" is focused by default and Esc stays too. |
| Browser Back/Forward | The same dialog; the URL doesn't change until you choose to leave. |
| Reload, closing the tab, typing a URL | The browser's own "Leave site?" prompt. Browsers don't allow a custom dialog there. |

"Leave without saving" follows the UX plan: an outlined red button, never a filled one. The dialog is a new shared `AlertDialog` in `src/components/ui/`, built on the existing `radix-ui` package with no new dependency. The guard itself is a reusable hook, `use-unsaved-changes-guard.ts`, so other forms can use it later.

**A bug the new test caught:** after saving and pressing Back, every setup screen showed the version from before the save, because Next kept its cached copy of the page. The saves did persist; the display was stale. Saving records, adding or removing a vendor, saving rules and changing the threshold now clear that cache, so Back shows the current state.

**Tradeoffs:**
- **Extra Back press:** catching Back needs one extra history entry. If you undo your edits and then press Back, it takes two presses to leave.
- **Opened in a new tab:** if the Rules page was the tab's first page, Back has nowhere to go, so choosing "Leave without saving" after Back does nothing.
- **Sign-out isn't guarded:** it's a button, not a link, so signing out drops unsaved rule edits without asking. Guarding it too is easy if you want it.

The new e2e spec checks each case: a clean form navigates freely; a link or Back asks, and Stay keeps the edit; closing the tab gets the browser's prompt; Leave discards the edit; and saving then Back shows the saved rules.

I've built three palette pages and a compare page. Each palette shows every component in dark and light, and every color pair passes the contrast standard in both themes. Your server on port 3000 (PID 38148) is already serving them:

- **Compare all three:** http://localhost:3000/palettes/index.html. Each is rendered at desktop width side by side, with one Dark/Light switch for all three.
- http://localhost:3000/palettes/coral-slate.html
- http://localhost:3000/palettes/indigo-ledger.html
- http://localhost:3000/palettes/graphite-teal.html

**Why dark mode looks dull now:** it's the neutrals, not the coral. Every dark gray in the current theme is warm brown: page `#171413`, cards `#211d1b`, borders `#50433c`, secondary text a pinkish beige `#c2aea3`, and even the "selected" color is brown `#3b2520`. That amber cast is exactly what Windows Night Light and Android's night mode apply. On top of that, the card and page backgrounds are so close that cards don't separate from the page. All three options use cool, near-neutral grays, text at about 16–17:1 contrast, and clearly visible card edges.

**What's on each page:**
- **Mock app:** the header, the side steps and a Results screen with the category tiles, filters, and a results table with selected, hover, failed and pending rows.
- **Components:** buttons, form controls in normal, focus and error states, badges, notices, toasts, the unsaved-changes dialog, run progress, rule cards, pair detail, menus, tabs, empty and loading states, and charts.
- **Reference:** every color value, and a contrast report listing each color pair the components use with its measured ratio.

| | Brand color | Feel |
|---|---|---|
| **Coral × Slate** | Your coral, brighter in dark (`#FF6A4D`), on blue-slate grays | Keeps your brand, energetic, still businesslike |
| **Indigo Ledger** | Indigo on deep navy | Classic "trusted analytics" (Stripe, Linear) |
| **Graphite × Teal** | Teal on graphite | Clinical precision, calm |

## My recommendation: Coral × Slate

1. **It fixes the actual problem without throwing away your brand.** The dullness came from the brown grays; this swaps them for slate and keeps the wordmark, the coral and the color system you already adopted.
2. **It stands out.** Nearly every data or B2B tool is blue, indigo or teal. Coral reads as "signal" and "insight", which fits the name PairSight, and it stays professional because it only marks actions and selection on neutral surfaces.
3. **It suits the product's main message.** PairSight shows agreement, not correctness. With teal, the main action color sits near the green used for "succeeded", which hints at "right". Coral carries no right/wrong meaning.

**Its one risk:** coral is close to error red. I kept error red clearly pink (a different hue), made destructive buttons outlined rather than filled, and every error has an icon. Check that side by side and see if it holds up for you.

**Choose Indigo Ledger** if you want the safest, most conventional "enterprise trust" look; it's the calmest of the three. **Choose Graphite × Teal** if you want it clinical. There, the "Vendor only" category moves from teal to orange so it doesn't clash with the teal brand.

Nothing is committed. The pages are untracked files in `public/palettes/` for review only. Once you pick one (or ask for a mix, such as Coral's brand with Indigo's navy background), I'll move it into `globals.css`, update the contrast test and `DESIGN.md`, and delete the preview pages.

Poppins is now the app's font, and JetBrains Mono is the second font. JetBrains Mono is the monospace font in your second screenshot, and the app already loaded it. Every screen now uses one shared set of text styles. Nothing is committed.

**How the two fonts are split:**
- **Poppins** (regular, medium, semibold) for everything you read as interface: headings, labels, body text, buttons, category names.
- **JetBrains Mono** for data you read exactly, not skim.

**Where the mono appears, and why it stands out there:**
1. **Rules (your example):** each rule card now shows its key under the friendly title, as mono chips such as `name` + `postcode` or `last_name` + `postcode`. The title is the label, the chips are the exact definition. The stats line underneath is mono too.
2. **Pair detail:** the raw values in both records, so `Smith` vs `Smyth` compares letter by letter.
3. **Column mapping:** the CSV headers and sample rows.
4. **Numbers in columns or live counters:** Jev scores, plan counts, run counters, the threshold input. Poppins has no fixed-width digits (a 1 is half as wide as a 0), so in Poppins these columns misalign and live counters jitter. Numbers inside sentences ("Run 2 · 8 pairs") stay in Poppins.
5. **Record references and file names.**
6. **Keyboard hints:** `J` `K` `Esc` as key caps on the pair page.

**Consistency:** before, section headings came in four different sizes. Now every screen uses one set of named styles (`type-title`, `type-heading`, `type-label`, `type-data`, and so on) instead of raw sizes. That covers all the screens and the shared components (tables, dialogs, inputs, toasts). Table headers are now small uppercase labels, and screen titles all match. The full table is in `DESIGN.md` §4.

**Problems I found and fixed while reviewing every screen:**
- The column-role dropdowns and "Suggested" chips in column mapping were rendering bold and uppercase.
- "8 of 8 pairs judged" was wrongly set in the mono.
- The results search box cut off its placeholder, since Poppins is wider than Inter.
- The run progress bar had a maroon track, the same brand-tint problem as before, and didn't report its value to screen readers.

**Test changes:**
- **Stray dev servers:** local PairSight e2e runs no longer reuse a server that's already running. Playwright's leftover server had nearly routed test runs to the real TypeSafe API several times; now a busy port fails the run instead.
- **Flaky spec:** one spec failed under parallel load because it didn't wait for pages to finish loading after a click. It now waits, and passes when run three copies at once.

**Checks:** lint, typecheck, format, the build and 9 of 9 e2e specs pass; the unit tests show only the 10 known Windows-only failures. I checked every screen visually in dark mode.

The dev server isn't running; `pnpm dev` will show it. When you're happy, I'll commit this as a typography commit, the color theme and the e2e fixes as their own commits, and the session log separately.

I've built three layout variants and a compare page. All three use the same two screens and the same spacing, so only the app structure differs. The dev server on port 3000 is serving them:

- **Compare:** http://localhost:3000/layouts/index.html. Switch Results/Rules and Dark/Light for all three at once.
- http://localhost:3000/layouts/workspace-sidebar.html
- http://localhost:3000/layouts/top-bar-tabs.html
- http://localhost:3000/layouts/rail-context.html

Each page switches between Results and Rules when you click those steps, so you can see how a shell behaves on a dense table and on a long form.

**Why it feels like a static page now:** the steps sit in a floating box, the header is just a strip of links, and there are no fixed app surfaces or places for page actions. All three variants fix that the same way:
- anchored navigation surfaces
- a 56px top bar or header
- a page header with actions, not just a title
- live context (step counts, run status)
- a consistent grid: 32px page padding, 24px between sections, 20px inside cards, 36px controls and nav items, 48px table rows

| | Structure | Best at |
|---|---|---|
| **A · Workspace sidebar** | Full-height sidebar with brand, search, global nav, the current evaluation's steps with counts, and your account at the bottom. A top bar with breadcrumb and global actions. | Feeling like a real product; the step flow is always visible |
| **B · Top bar + step tabs** | Global top bar; an evaluation header with name, status and the six steps as tabs; full-width content | Maximum width for wide tables |
| **C · Icon rail + context panel** | 64px icon rail, a 264px evaluation panel where each step shows a one-line summary, and a sticky page header | Rich context, for dense multi-module tools |

## My recommendation: A · Workspace sidebar

1. **It fixes exactly what you described.** The sidebar is anchored full-height with its own surface, and brand, nav, workflow and account all have fixed places. That's what makes Linear, Vercel and Stripe feel like apps rather than pages.
2. **It matches how PairSight works.** An evaluation is a six-step workflow you move back and forth through. A vertical list of steps with live counts (`24` records, `3 on`, `1,344` pairs) shows progress at a glance. B's tabs squeeze the counts; C spends two columns on it.
3. **It scales without redesign.** New sections go in the sidebar, and page actions (Run selector, Re-run, Threshold) get a consistent home in the page header.
4. **The cost is width.** The sidebar takes 256px from the Results table. At 1,440px the table still has plenty of room, and the collapse button (top of the sidebar) can shrink it to icons when you need width.

**Runner-up: B**, if full-width tables matter most to you. I'd skip C: an icon rail pays off when there are many top-level sections, and PairSight has two or three, so here it's a second panel without enough to fill it.

**Illustrative elements:** some things in the previews show where features would go and aren't built yet: search (⌘K), notifications, the Guide link, the Re-run button, "Saved 2 min ago", and the collapse button. I'd only build what you choose.

Nothing is committed. The previews are temporary files in `public/layouts/`, and I'll delete them once you've picked one (or a mix, such as A's sidebar with C's step summaries).
