Here's everything since the last devlog list (1 Oct, 22:54). That list ended with the Phase 6 landing redesign done but not yet committed, so this picks up from there.

## 1 Oct: closing out M3 (PR #5, merged)
- Committed Phase 6: the landing, sign-in and not-found page redesign.
- **Phase 7 clean-up:**
  - Removed the Magic Patterns leftovers and retired components: IconTile, EmptyState, Badge (replaced by Chip), Section, and two old Button variants.
  - Deleted five unused shadcn components, five unused type styles, the duotone icons, and 37 unused translation keys.
  - Updated `DESIGN.md`, the component and icon rules, and the UX plan's "What shipped" section, which records how all 20 design differences were settled.
- Opened PR #5 ("finish M3 and rebuild the UI to the final design") after a full e2e run (17/17). You merged it.

## 2 Oct: M4, the sandbox (PR #6, merged)
- **Demo became a sandbox:** `/sandbox` lets visitors walk the whole app read-only with no login, on one fictional evaluation (24 records, a pair-format and a group-format vendor).
- **Real Jev snapshot:** recorded once with your key; `pnpm demo:snapshot` and `pnpm demo:seed` load it, and running the seed twice gives the same result.
- **Access:** a new `EvaluationScope` controls who can read an evaluation (the owner, or anyone for the sandbox). Every change still requires the owner.
- **One set of screens:** each page became a shared screen used by both `/evaluations/[id]` and `/sandbox`, and links are built from a base path.
- **Read-only:** save, upload and run controls show a "Sign up to …" tip. There's a sandbox banner, the sidebar shows Sign up / Sign in when signed out, and the landing, auth pages and empty dashboard link to it.
- **Opening an evaluation:** it now goes to wherever the evaluation is up to. Results opens only once the latest run is complete; otherwise you land on the active step.
- **Copy:** the "fictional or anonymized data only" warnings are gone, replaced by a plain "Jev, TypeSafe's AI, judges each pair". The V1 scope now records the on-premises stance.
- **Motion everywhere:**
  - Landing: staged hero entrance, gradient title, tilting product preview, drifting glows, and sections that play when scrolled into view.
  - App: sections cascade in on each page, counters count up, score bars fill.
  - Results charts and cards slowed down so you can watch them build.
  - Reduced motion is respected.
- **Fixes:** the cards in the vendor and rules columns were touching; horizontal scroll on most pages (the action bar was a scrollbar too wide); phone overflow; a hidden label escaping a table; the pair page's vendor list wrapping; F1 running into the next number on the dashboard.
- 21/21 e2e passing.

## 2 Oct: M4 sign-off (PR #7, merged)
- **Design review:** 42 screens and states captured in light and dark at 1440, 1024 and 390 px. Fixes from it:
  - Layouts now respond to the room the page actually has (container queries), so 1024 px windows no longer break.
  - The Plan's pair count now adds up (rule matches + vendor pairs − duplicates = unique pairs).
  - Run states tell the truth: "Finished with N incomplete pairs", correct stopped and in-progress states.
  - Toasts no longer cover the action bar, and phone layouts were reordered.
  - Text wraps instead of truncating; plurals, zero cases and number formatting fixed; colours and the score distribution corrected.
- **Accessibility:** added axe checks on every public and sandbox screen in both themes, fixed what they found, and did a keyboard-only pass.
- **Your decisions:** the focus ring is now blue (`--focus`, a GitHub-style ring), and the status chip says "Ready to plan".
- **Minor fixes:** emails wrap at sensible points, the vendor step's wording, the phone action bar layout, and the dialog placeholder.
- **New e2e:** `public.spec.ts` (theme survives reload with no flash, 404, axe), sign-out, an invalid evaluation id, and three vendors (new Vendor C fixture).
- **Template:** `example.prisma` renamed to `user.prisma` (no migration), and the smoke test removed.
- **Settled values:** the low-confidence band stays ±0.1 and the default threshold 0.50, recorded in the docs.
- **Threshold in Rules:**
  - Lenient 0.35, Balanced 0.50 or Strict 0.70, plus a custom value from 0.01 to 0.99.
  - Plan shows the chosen threshold, and each run keeps its own.
  - On Results it's preview-only (no Apply).
  - A threshold-only re-run reuses every score, so it finishes instantly with no Jev calls.
- **Runs centre:**
  - Run is no longer a pipeline step; the five steps are Records → Vendors → Rules → Plan → Results.
  - A Runs dialog on every page has Active and Completed tabs. Stopped runs show in Completed with a failed look, the reason and Resume.
  - Each card's details list threshold, judgments, model, vendors, rules and timing.
  - Animated cards and dialogs, and proper empty states.
  - The run loop moved into the app shell, so a run continues on any page.
- 36/36 e2e passing.

## 2 Oct: `feat/polish` (5 commits, not pushed)
- Removed the template's `/admin` and `/analytics-demo` pages.
- Docs rewritten to describe what v1 shipped: Product Overview, V1 Scope, the roadmap (Built / Next / Deferred) and the README.
- **Landing:** parallax illustrated "How it works" cards, a new "A closer look" section, and a coral gradient call-to-action panel.
- **Glass UI style** beside Classic, chosen under Theme → Style. You kept the subtle version, and Classic stays the default.

## 2 Oct: exports and Excel (`feat/polish`, uncommitted, what you're testing now)
- **Shared tabular connector** (`src/server/pairsight/tabular/`): every import and export goes through it. Each format is one adapter, and the checks are shared. Uploads now send the file itself rather than its text.
- **Findings export:** one row per pair with the mapped fields, Jev's status, score and model, PairSight's decision, the low-confidence flag, what found the pair, and each vendor's decision and category. It works in the sandbox signed out, someone else's run gets a 404, and downloads are never cached.
- **CSV:** UTF-8 with a BOM, and cells that would run as formulas are made inert.
- **Excel import:** `.xlsx` for records and vendor files. Dates, numbers and formulas are read as you see them. A workbook with several sheets of data shows a sheet picker, and the file bar names the sheet read. Corrupt or oversized workbooks are refused safely.
- **Excel export:** an Export menu (CSV / Excel). The workbook has a bold, frozen, filterable header, sized columns, and the score as a number.
- 37/37 e2e passing (a new Excel round-trip test), 264 unit tests passing.

The PDF summary report is built: the Export menu now has a third item, "Summary report (.pdf)", alongside CSV and Excel. All 37 e2e tests pass and 270 unit tests pass (the 10 failures are the usual Windows-only standards self-tests). Nothing is committed.

The sandbox's report is 5 A4 pages:
1. **Verdict and scorecard:** "On these 24 records, Clearline Resolve matched most like Jev's judgment (F1 0.50)". It also says the result holds at every threshold, so it doesn't depend on where the threshold was set. When different vendors are best on wrong merges or on missed duplicates, a "Fewest wrong merges: …" or "Fewest missed duplicates: …" line names them. The scorecard ranks every vendor with matched pairs, agreed with Jev, wrong merges, missed duplicates, precision, recall and F1, each explained in plain business terms.
2. **Tradeoffs:**
   - What each vendor gets wrong, and which fields those pairs usually share or differ on.
   - A chart of each vendor's F1 at every threshold.
   - A head-to-head sentence, e.g. "Where only one of Northwind Match and Clearline Resolve matched a pair, Jev sided with Clearline Resolve in 3 of 5." It's built for the analyst justifying a choice to their boss.
3. **Examples (pages 3–4):** each vendor's clearest wrong merges and missed duplicates. Each one shows the names, Jev's score, how the pair was found, and every field side by side marked same, different or missing.
4. **Method and scope:** the files, the fields sent to Jev, the identity definition, rules and coverage. It includes "268 possible pairs were never compared", the Jev model, run times, scores reused versus judged in this run, "Human review: none", and a glossary.

Every page has a footer, and the caveat that these figures are AI-estimated agreement with Jev, not verified accuracy, is on the first page. It works signed out in the sandbox, and anyone else's run gets a 404.

Two things differ from the plan:
- **Font:** the app gets Poppins from Google at build time, so there's no font file to embed. The PDF uses the built-in Helvetica, so nothing has to be bundled and it renders the same in every PDF reader.
- **One rendering quirk:** react-pdf drops page numbers when line height is set on the page, so it's set on each text style instead. There's a comment on it in `styles.ts`.

Coverage:
- **Unit tests:** 6 new ones in `report-summary.test.mts` cover ranking, the safest and most complete vendors, whether the lead holds across thresholds, head-to-head, examples and field comparisons.
- **E2E:** the demo flow downloads and reads the PDF and checks that a signed-out request gets a 404. The sandbox test checks both vendors' ranking and the head-to-head line.
- **Visual check:** I've only looked at the sandbox's two-vendor report. The one- and three-vendor reports are covered by text checks in e2e but I haven't looked at them as PDFs; worth downloading one from your own runs.

**To review:** open the sandbox, go to Results → Export → Summary report. The copy I reviewed is at `C:\Users\onais\AppData\Local\Temp\claude\C--t1-naisxx\90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137\scratchpad\sandbox-summary.pdf`. Your dev server on :3000 will pick it up after a restart, since there are two new dependencies (`@react-pdf/renderer`, and `pdf-parse` for tests only).

The UX plan, roadmap (PDF moved to Built), V1 scope, Product Overview, README and `DESIGN.md` are updated. After you've reviewed it, I'll run local-review and e2e-review again and commit in feature groups.

I fixed the page 3 overlap: example cards that reach the bottom of a page now move whole to the next page instead of piling onto each other.

**Cause:** react-pdf lays out a "keep together" block wrongly at a page break when it sits inside other wrappers. The cards were inside a section and a vendor wrapper, so they collapsed at the bottom of the page. My previous change made it more likely.

**Fix:** in `src/server/pairsight/report-pdf/`, every example card and its heading now sit directly on the page with no wrappers. The section heading is part of its first card, so it moves with it and can't be left alone at the bottom.

**Checked:** I couldn't download a fresh copy from the app because Docker Desktop isn't running, so the database is down. Organisations and products are built on `feat/entity-types-and-field-dictionary`. The full e2e suite passes 40 of 40 on a fresh build (including new organisation, product and sandbox-switch tests). Unit tests pass apart from the usual 10 Windows-only failures, and lint, typecheck, format, env and secrets are clean. Nothing is committed yet.

**Live Jev check (14 calls, fictional data):** the 0.50 threshold works for both new types, so they share it with people.
- **Organisations:** same company 0.85–0.88, a company that moved offices 0.63; parent/subsidiary, a shared address and same-name companies 0.05–0.08.
- **Products:** same product 0.88–0.97; size or colour variants, a 6-pack and same-name different brands 0.05–0.08.

**What to test**
- **Records:** upload a file and PairSight says "These look like organisations" (or people, or products) with a three-way picker; if the file is ambiguous it asks instead. Switching the type re-suggests that type's fields. Try `e2e/fixtures/company-source.csv` or `prisma/demo/sandbox-products.csv`.
- **Auto-mapping:** a much larger English dictionary per type (CRM, ERP and shop export names) plus a check of sample values. A column of emails, websites or barcodes gets mapped even with a meaningless name, and old false matches like "Hotel" → phone and "Last updated" → last name are gone. About 120 real column names are covered by tests.
- **Rules:** each type has its own presets and defaults.
  - **Organisations:** website domain, registration number and name + postcode; legal forms like "Ltd" are ignored when comparing names.
  - **Products:** barcode (UPC and EAN of the same item count as one), part number + brand and name + brand.
  - The definition of "the same" is type-specific too.
- **Results, pairs and exports:** organisations show their name, products show brand + name. Copy that said "same person" now says "judged the same".
- **Dashboard:** each evaluation shows its type.
- **Sandbox:** the banner switches between People, Organisations and Products. I recorded the two new sandboxes with real Jev runs, 13 pairs each.

**Things you should know**
- **People are unaffected:** a person evaluation sends Jev exactly the same question and uses the same judgment key as before, so existing scores and the person sandbox stay valid. A test pins this.
- **Migration:** one generated migration adds the type to evaluations and runs. Older evaluations read as people.
- **Sandbox choice is a cookie, not the URL:** a URL parameter would have broken every sandbox link and the run pages.
- **Replacing records with another kind of file** resets the rules and the definition to that type's defaults. The replace dialog now warns about this, but no e2e test covers it.
- **Not done from the plan:** hiding the country field for products, and normalising dates of birth to ISO.
- **Local leftovers:** the recordings left two `sandbox-recorder+…@pairsight.test` accounts in your local database.

To try it, restart your dev server. The migration and `pnpm demo:seed` have already run on your local database.

When you're happy, tell me and I'll run local-review and e2e-review again and commit in groups.

To try it, restart your dev server and export from any run's Results, or from the sandbox. The PDF takes the longest, so it shows the progress best.

# Prompt audit

**What I checked:** your agent instructions, skills and commands, the rule files they point to, the Claude review workflow, and the code that calls a model. Nothing was edited.

**Which model they're judged against:**
- **Instruction files, skills, commands:** Claude Opus 5.5. That's the model running this audit; nothing in the repo pins one for them.
- **App prompt (`src/lib/pairsight/entity-types.ts`, `src/server/pairsight/judgment.ts`, `src/server/ai/typesafe.ts`):** these go to TypeSafe's Jev, not Claude. I only checked them for general problems and propose nothing provider-specific.
- **`src/server/ai/anthropic.ts`:** an unused placeholder.

**Skipped:** settings, `.env*` and MCP files (they can hold secrets), and `~/.claude/extract-log.py` (outside the project).

## Summary

Your instruction files are in good shape. There's almost no shouting, no "think step by step", no retired model names, and no word caps or update suppressors. Every path and `pnpm` script they name exists.

The problems are facts the repo has outgrown since it was set up from the template:

1. **Migrations:** four template files still say "CI generates migrations". PairSight generates them locally and commits them, as `docs/rules/migrations.md` and `AGENTS.md` (both newer) say. The riskiest is `e2e-build`, which tells an agent "do NOT hand-write a migration — CI generates it". An agent following it would ship a schema change with no migration.
2. **`seed-for-pr`:** its command can't work here. `pnpm tsx` isn't installed, and a script that imports the app's `db` crashes on load outside Next.
3. **`docs/rules/authorization.md`:** it describes an admin layout that mounts `<AuthzProvider>`, but that layout was removed in `abf3f61`.

**Findings by group:**
- **Group 1, outdated prompt wording:** 1 (low, flag)
- **Group 2, stale or conflicting instruction files:** 8 (3 high with proposed edits, 5 flags)
- **Group 3, tool descriptions:** not applicable (the app gives no model any tools)
- **Group 4, request code:** 1 (low, flag)

## Findings

| # | Location | Evidence | Pattern | Why it's out of date | Confidence | Action |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `docs/rules/authorization.md:49` | "`requireAdmin()` — any active seat (the coarse gate the admin layout uses)." | G2 Volatile specifics | `src/app/[locale]/admin/layout.tsx` was deleted in `abf3f61`, and nothing in `src/` calls `requireAdmin` now. | High | `rewrite` (diff 1) |
| 2 | `docs/rules/authorization.md:80-83` | "mounts `<AuthzProvider permissions={…}>` in the **admin route-group layout**" | G2 Volatile specifics | Same layout. `<AuthzProvider>` is defined in `src/lib/rbac/authz-provider.tsx` but mounted nowhere. | High | `rewrite` (diff 2) |
| 3 | `.claude/skills/seed-for-pr/SKILL.md:17-21` | "imports `db` from `@/server/db/client`" … "`pnpm tsx scripts/seed-<name>.mjs`" | G2 Volatile specifics | `tsx` isn't a dependency. `src/server/db/client.ts:1` imports `'server-only'`, which throws in a plain Node script. The pattern that works here is `scripts/demo-seed.mts`: its own `new PrismaClient()`, run through `scripts/register-ts.mjs`. | High | `rewrite` (diff 3) |

The rules card now shows how blocking rules combine: OR between rules, AND within a rule. The full e2e suite passed 40/40, and typecheck and lint are clean. Nothing is committed.

**What changed on the Rules screen:**
- **A note under the card heading** with two short definitions:
  - **Between rules (OR):** "A pair is compared when any switched-on rule finds it. One matching rule is enough."
  - **Within a rule (AND):** "Every field in the rule must match. A pair that matches only some of them isn't found by that rule."
- **OR dividers** sit between every rule in the list, including before your own composite rules. That makes the "any one rule is enough" behaviour visible without reading the note.
- **AND chips** appear under any rule that uses more than one field. For example, "Same name and postcode" now shows `Name AND Postcode`. They're greyed out when the rule is off. Your own composite rules use the same chips instead of the old "+".

I've fixed the header showing "Running" after a run finishes, and the full e2e suite passed 40/40. Nothing is committed.

**Cause:** when a run finished with the Runs dialog open, the app did two things back to back. It asked the page to refresh, then moved you to the results page. In Next.js, moving to a new page cancels any refresh still in progress. The results page loaded fresh, but the header belongs to the shared layout around it. That layout kept the "Running" from before.

**Fix:** in `src/components/pairsight/runs/runs-centre.tsx` the refresh now happens after the move to results. Next.js runs it once the move completes, so the header updates to "Complete", or "Needs attention" if some pairs couldn't be judged. The "View results" button on the completion toast was never affected.

**Test:** the e2e step that starts a run now also checks the header after the dialog closes. It expects "Complete" or "Needs attention", not "Running". It passes with the fix. I didn't rebuild the old code to watch it fail first, so it hasn't been shown to catch the bug.

I've stopped the test servers again and cleared the e2e accounts' `ai_usage` rows. Your :3000 dev server should pick up the change, so another live run should show the header correct after the redirect.

I've tightened the type detection so a file with a plain person-name column is read as people. I couldn't reproduce "Full Name" mapping to Company Name exactly, though, so it's worth a quick look with your file. Nothing is committed.

**What I found:** "Full Name" never matches Company Name, under any type. Within one type, the mapping always uses that type's own suggestions. So the only way to get a Company Name suggestion was for the file to be detected as organisations. I found one way a people file gets there: it has an employer column whose values end in "Ltd", "Inc", "GmbH" and so on. Those endings count as strong evidence of organisations, enough to outweigh the "Full Name" column. Once the type flips, any "Name" or "Customer Name" column is mapped to Company Name.

**Fix** (`src/lib/pairsight/field-dictionary.ts`):
- A column plainly named for a person ("Full Name", "First Name", "Surname", "Date of Birth") now counts as evidence of people.
- When such a column is present, "Acme Ltd" values in another column are read as an employer, not as a sign the file is organisations.
- Names like "Contact Name" or "Customer Name" don't count, because company files often have a contact person. A company export with a "Contact Name" column is still detected as organisations.

Each of those cases now has a unit test. The dictionary tests pass, the full unit suite has the same 284 passes and the same 10 Windows-only failures, and typecheck and lint are clean. I didn't rerun e2e this time: the company and product test files have no person-name columns, so their detection doesn't change.

If you still have the file that misbehaved, could you send its column headers (just the names, no data)? If it didn't have a company-style column, this fix won't cover it, and the headers will show me what did.

Here's everything since the last devlog list (2 Oct, 20:43). That list ended with exports and Excel import on `feat/polish`, uncommitted while you tested. This picks up from there.

## 3 Oct: PDF summary report and PR #8 (merged)
- **Summary report** in the Export menu, built for an analyst who has to justify a vendor choice to their boss:
  - Which vendor matched most like Jev, and whether that holds across thresholds.
  - What each vendor gets wrong.
  - A head to head between the vendors ("Jev sided with X in N of M").
  - Example pairs with names and key fields.
  - The method and scope behind the numbers.
  - It's drawn on the server with `@react-pdf/renderer`. It works in the sandbox signed out, and someone else's run gets a 404.
- **Page 3 layout fix:** cards that weren't allowed to split across pages were nested inside each other, which broke the layout. They now sit directly on the page.
- The branch was renamed from `feat/polish` to `feat/import-export-and-polish`. Local review and full e2e passed, then PR #8 was opened ("CSV and Excel import/export, a PDF summary report, landing and Glass polish"). You merged it.

## 3 Oct: organisations and products (`feat/entity-types-and-field-dictionary`)
- **Exploration finding:** Jev's API has no notion of record type. It's always "two records plus one yes/no question", so everything person-specific was PairSight's own code.
- **Three record types:** people, organisations and products, with one registry (`entity-types.ts`) holding what differs per type:
  - **Fields:** 27 in all.
    - People: name, email, phone, date of birth, address.
    - Organisations: company and trading name, registration number, tax ID, website, industry.
    - Products: name, brand, manufacturer, GTIN, MPN, SKU, model, category, colour, size.
  - **Blocking presets and defaults per type:**
    - Organisations: website domain, registration number, name + postcode.
    - Products: GTIN, MPN + brand, name + brand.
  - **The Jev question and its criteria per type.** Parent vs. subsidiary counts as different organisations; size or colour variants and multipacks count as different products.
  - **New normalisers:**
    - Company names lose legal suffixes like Ltd, GmbH and Inc.
    - Websites reduce to the domain.
    - GTINs are validated by check digit.
    - Codes are uppercased with punctuation removed.
  - **People unchanged:** the person flow is unchanged, so old judgments and the sandbox recording stay valid.
- **Database:** an `EntityType` enum, `Evaluation.entityType` and `Run.entityType` (existing runs default to person), with a generated migration.
- **Type detected from the upload:** a "These look like…" picker on Records, which asks when unsure. Switching type re-suggests the mapping without another upload. Replacing a file with a different type resets that evaluation's rules and identity text.
- **Much bigger field dictionary** (`field-synonyms.ts` for the data, `field-dictionary.ts` for the logic):
  - It covers column names from common CRM and ERP exports (Salesforce, HubSpot, Dynamics, SAP, Shopify).
  - Matches are by whole word only, which ended the old false hits like "hotel" → phone and "ethnicity" → city.
  - It understands qualifiers, so "Billing Postcode" → postcode.
  - Sample values confirm or overrule a guess: emails, phones, dates, websites, valid GTINs. A column named "col7" full of emails gets suggested as email.
  - It has its own unit tests.
- **Live Jev check** on fictional pairs, recorded in V1 Scope:
  - Organisations: same 0.85–0.88 (a moved office 0.63), different 0.05–0.08.
  - Products: same 0.88–0.97, different 0.05–0.08.
  - 0.50 holds for every type.
- **Stub and scripts:** the Jev stub scores each type, and `jev-check` and `demo-seed` handle all three.

## 3 Oct: changes from your testing (same branch)
- **Export progress:** an export shows a "Preparing…" toast while the file is generated, then "Download started" or an error. The button shows a spinner meanwhile.
- **Sandbox as three evaluations:** you asked for evaluations instead of a type switch. The sandbox dashboard now lists Customer master, Supplier master and Product catalogue side by side, each opening at `/sandbox/[id]/…`. All three use real recorded Jev runs: organisations 13 pairs (8 the same), products 13 pairs (5 the same).
- **Prompt audit** (`/claude-api prompt-audit`): report and proposed diff delivered, not applied. Main findings:
  - Stale admin-layout references in the authorization rules.
  - The `seed-for-pr` skill calls `pnpm tsx`, which doesn't exist.
  - Several "CI generates migrations" lines that contradict local generation.
  - The cicd command still asks for screenshots, against your no-screenshots preference.
- **AND/OR on blocking rules:**
  - A blue **OR** tag on dashed dividers between rules.
  - Amber **AND** tags between the fields of a multi-field rule.
  - Both are in JetBrains Mono.
  - A "How rules combine" hover explainer replaces the inline box.
  - Rule labels everywhere, including exports and the PDF, now read "Email AND Postcode".
- **Bug fix, "Running" after a finished run:** with the Runs dialog open, moving to Results cancelled the header's refresh, so it kept saying "Running". The refresh now happens after the move, and an e2e check covers it.
- **Bug fix, a people file read as organisations:** an employer column with "Acme Ltd"-style values outweighed "Full Name". Now a plainly person-named column ("Full Name", "Surname", "DOB") means people. "Contact Name" still doesn't flip a company file.

## 3 Oct: review and commits
- `/local-review`:
  - All gates passed, including the RLS check (15 tables) and the production build.
  - `check:standards` meets 17 of 24 and 10 unit tests fail; both come from Windows (no exec bit, and the script can't launch `eslint`/`tsc`), not this branch.
  - The manual pass found nothing.
- `/e2e-review`: 40/40 passing.
- 7 commits, not pushed:
  - records support for organisations and products
  - three sandbox evaluations
  - export progress
  - AND/OR rules
  - the top bar fix
  - e2e coverage
  - docs
- The session log stays uncommitted until the push.

Here's my take. I've kept it to the decisions that matter, and I'd like to compare it with your research before we plan anything.

## How I'd frame it

In PairSight, blocking isn't there to find duplicates. It decides which pairs Jev gets to judge, and every vendor is scored only against that set. That makes our blind spot a duplicate that our rules miss *and* no vendor matched. That pair never shows up anywhere, so every vendor's recall looks better than it is.

Today's rules are exact matches on cleaned-up values (email, phone, name + postcode). They catch easy duplicates and miss the realistic ones, like "Jon Smyth, 12 Oak St" against "John Smith, 12 Oak Street" with different emails. The vendors are fuzzy matchers, so they find exactly the pairs we can't. Our candidate set is tilted toward easy pairs, and the hard ones only appear when a vendor brings them. Fuzzy blocking is how we measure the vendors on the hard cases. It's about how fair the evaluation is, not a nice-to-have.

## Derived fields and fuzzy blocking are one feature at two strengths

**1. Derived rule fields: exact match on a computed key.** This is the classic, cheap form of fuzzy blocking:

| Type | Derived keys worth having |
|---|---|
| People | surname sound-alike code (Double Metaphone, so Smith = Smyth), first initial, first name sound-alike, year of birth, email username, email domain*, phone last 7 digits, postcode area or district |
| Organisations | the name's words in sorted order (so "Traders Brightwater Ltd" = "Brightwater Traders"), the name's first word, the name's initials, website domain, postcode district |
| Products | brand + model digits, GTIN without packaging digit, the name's sorted words, MPN with no separators |

\*Email domain alone is useless (every Gmail address in one bucket). It only works combined with something else, which our AND already allows.

These fit everything we already have: buckets, the Buckets/Pairs/Largest stats, the AND/OR display and "found by". A rule like "Surname sounds like AND Year of birth" explains itself.

**2. Similarity rules: "names at least X% similar".** This is real fuzzy blocking, where pairs are compared rather than grouped into buckets. The usual objection is cost, because you can't compare every pair. **At our scale that isn't true.** With 500 records there are about 125k possible pairs, and scoring all of them with Jaro-Winkler or word-overlap similarity takes milliseconds on the server. Compute isn't the limit; the 5,000-pair Jev budget is. So the clever indexing techniques the literature is full of (sorted neighbourhood, LSH, MinHash) solve a problem we don't have yet.

## What I'd recommend

**Phase 1: derived keys (most of the value, low risk)**
- In the composite rule editor, each field gets an optional "match on…" choice: the field as it is, sounds like, first N letters, initials, words in any order, domain, username, year, last N digits. Each choice is one small pure function next to `normalize.ts`.
- Each type gets one or two new presets, off by default: for people, "Surname sounds like AND Year of birth"; for organisations, "Name words AND Postcode district".
- Labels say what's derived: `Surname (sounds like) AND Birth year`.
- **Never** offer a derived key on its own if it gives huge buckets (initial alone, domain alone, birth year alone). Either require AND with another field, or block the rule when the plan's stats show a giant bucket.

**Phase 2: one similarity rule per type, capped by budget**
- "Similar name" with a threshold (say Jaro-Winkler ≥ 0.9), optionally AND an exact field ("Similar name AND Same postcode district").
- The plan shows how many pairs it adds. If that blows the 5,000 cap, it says so rather than cutting pairs silently. That's the rule we already apply to vendor pairs.
- Both phases sit behind the same rule shape, so if we ever go past about 5k records and need a worker, only the inside of the similarity rule changes (to sorted neighbourhood or an index). The UI and the data stay the same.

**What I'd skip**
- **Embeddings or AI for blocking:** extra cost and an extra AI call before Jev, and hard to explain to an analyst. Jev is already the semantic judge, and blocking should stay cheap and predictable.
- **Learned blocking** (training rules from labelled pairs): we have no labels, by design.
- **Arbitrary user-written expressions:** a field plus a fixed menu of transforms covers it, and the results stay explainable.

## One thing it unlocks for the evaluation

Once fuzzy rules exist, "found by" can tell exact rules from fuzzy ones. The results could then show how many duplicates only the fuzzy rules found, and which vendors caught them. That's the clearest evidence an analyst can take to their boss about who handles hard cases, and it fits the PDF report naturally.

## What I'd want from your research

- Did it favour sound-alike codes (Soundex vs Double Metaphone), and does that matter outside English names?
- Any numbers on how much recall derived keys add over exact blocking? That would tell us whether Phase 2 is worth it.
- Your expected real file sizes. If customers will bring 50k-record files soon, the Phase 2 design changes, and the worker moves up the list.

Share what you've got and I'll fold it into a plan.

Good question. Your challenge exposes a weak spot in what I said: "we have no labels" was too quick. Here's the whole concept first, then where I was wrong and what really stands in the way.

## Blocking vs matching

Deduplication has two stages, and "learned" means different things in each:

| | Blocking | Matching |
|---|---|---|
| Question | "Which pairs are worth looking at?" | "Is this pair the same entity?" |
| Must be | Cheap and high-recall (miss nothing) | Accurate |
| In PairSight | Our rules | Jev |

"Features and labels" usually brings to mind **learned matching**: build features for each pair (name similarity, same email, birth-date gap…), label pairs match or non-match, train a classifier. That's what the vendors sell, and Jev's job here. We don't need a second matcher.

**Learned blocking** uses the same ingredients for a different output. It doesn't predict "match"; it **picks the blocking rules**.

## How learned blocking works

There are two families.

**1. Learning a blocking scheme (rule learning).** This is the classic form, and the one that fits us.
- **Building blocks:** candidate tests, each a field plus a transform. "Same surname sound", "same first 3 letters of name", "same postcode district", "same birth year", "same email domain"…
- **Features:** for each labelled pair, which tests fire (true/false per test).
- **Labels:** match or non-match for those pairs.
- **Goal:** pick a set of rules (OR between rules, AND within a rule) that:
  - catches as many known matches as possible (*pair completeness*, which is recall)
  - while producing as few pairs as possible across the *whole* file (*reduction ratio*, which is cost).
- **Algorithm:** usually greedy, like a set-cover problem. Repeatedly add the rule with the best ratio of newly caught matches to pairs it adds, and stop at a recall target or the pair budget.
- **Output:** exactly our rule shape, OR of ANDs. The result would look like ordinary rules the user can read and switch off. Nothing opaque.

Small example. Suppose the confirmed matches are:
- "Jon Smyth / John Smith, 1990"
- "Ann Lee / Anne Lee, 1985"
- "R. Patel / Raj Patel, 1979"

The learner might find that "surname sounds alike AND same birth year" catches all three for 40 extra pairs. "Same first name" catches one for 900 pairs, so it's rejected.

**2. Learned representations (deep-learning blocking).** Train a model that turns each record into a vector so that duplicates land close together, then pair each record with its nearest neighbours. Strong at scale, but it needs lots of labelled data and compute, and it's a black box: "these were near in vector space" isn't something an analyst can defend to their boss. That's the family I'd still skip.

## Where I was wrong: we *do* have labels

After a run, every judged pair has a Jev verdict. Those are labels. Noisy, since they're Jev's judgment rather than verified truth, but real. The best of them are the **vendor-only pairs that Jev confirmed**: duplicates our rules missed and a vendor found. Those are exactly the hard cases the learner needs.

## What actually stands in the way

1. **Cold start.** Run 1 has no labels, so learning can only *suggest rules for the next run*. That's a fine workflow ("PairSight suggests 2 rules from your last run"), but it can never be the first step.
2. **We only label what we already looked at.** Labels exist only for pairs our rules or a vendor already proposed. A duplicate nobody proposed is never labelled, so the learner can't learn to find that kind. Left alone, it learns to re-find what's already found. Two ways to break the loop:
   - the vendor-only confirmed pairs from point 1, and
   - **sampling pairs outside the rules** and sending a few to Jev, which is already on the deferred list. That's the only route to unbiased labels.
3. **Very few positives.** 500 records may hold only 20–60 confirmed duplicates. Learning from that few risks overfitting, a rule tuned to the quirks of those 30 pairs. That's manageable with simple rules (at most 2–3 tests, which our three-field limit already enforces) and by showing the evidence: "catches 18 of 21 confirmed duplicates, adds 140 pairs".
4. **It needs the building blocks first.** The learner picks from the candidate tests, which are the derived keys from Phase 1. With only exact fields, there's nothing interesting to learn. **So learned blocking isn't an alternative to derived keys; it's built on top of them.**
5. **Fairness between vendors.** If rules are learned mostly from Vendor A's finds, the candidate set leans toward A's kind of duplicate. Learning from all vendors' confirmed pairs together limits that, and the report should say the rules were suggested from the data.

## Revised position

I'd no longer skip it. I'd sequence it:
1. **Derived keys** (the tests).
2. **Sampling pairs outside the rules** (unbiased labels and a measure of the blind spot).
3. **Suggested rules from the last run's labels:** the greedy rule picker, with the evidence shown, and the user always accepting each rule. Never applied automatically.

I'd still skip the deep-learning kind, for the reasons above.

Here's one way to put your question into words:

> **"After this ships, what will the analyst be able to conclude that they can't today, and could it change which vendor we recommend?"**

The short answer is yes, it could change the recommendation. Here's why.

## What PairSight measures today, and where it's honest

Every vendor is scored on two things:

- **Precision:** "when the vendor says two records match, is it right?" That number is **already fair**. Every pair a vendor claims is always sent to Jev, whatever our rules say. Fuzzy blocking doesn't change it.
- **Recall:** "of all the real duplicates, how many did the vendor catch?" That number is **inflated today**. "All the real duplicates" can only mean the ones *someone* found: our rules or a vendor. A duplicate that nobody found isn't counted, so missing it costs no vendor anything.

So the problem isn't that results are wrong. They're **optimistic about recall, unevenly**, and the vendors that miss hard cases benefit most.

## A concrete before and after (made-up numbers)

A 500-customer file truly holds 130 duplicates:
- 100 easy ones: same email or phone. Our exact rules find these.
- 30 hard ones: "Jon Smyth" vs "John Smith", a new email, a moved address.

Vendor A catches 92 easy and 2 hard. Vendor B catches 85 easy and 24 hard.

**Today** (our rules see only the 100 easy ones, plus whatever the vendors bring):

| | Duplicates counted | A's recall | B's recall |
|---|---|---|---|
| Today | 100 easy + 24 hard (from B) = 124 | 94/124 = **76%** | 109/124 = **88%** |

Six hard duplicates found by neither vendor are invisible. B still looks better here, but the gap is understated and the report can't say how many duplicates all vendors missed.

**With derived keys** (for example "surname sounds alike AND birth year"), our rules now also find most of the hard 30:

| | Duplicates counted | A's recall | B's recall |
|---|---|---|---|
| After | all 130 | 94/130 = **72%** | 109/130 = **84%** |

**What changes for the analyst:**

1. **Honest recall.** Both numbers drop to their true level, and the drop is bigger for the vendor that misses hard cases.
2. **A new, persuasive list.** "6 duplicates every vendor missed", each with names and Jev's reasoning. That's the strongest slide for the boss. Today it can't exist.
3. **The hard-case story.** "On difficult duplicates (typos, moved address), B caught 24 of 30 and A caught 2." This is often *the* reason to pay for a vendor, and today PairSight can't show it unless one vendor happened to find those pairs.
4. **The verdict can flip.** In a file where A is better on easy cases and B on hard ones, today's numbers lean toward A. With the hard cases visible, B may come out ahead.

## What each piece adds

| Piece | What gets better for the analyst |
|---|---|
| **Derived keys** | Hard duplicates enter the evaluation, so recall is honest and "everyone missed these" becomes visible |
| **Sampling pairs outside the rules** | The report can state its own limits: "We estimate about 4 duplicates remain outside every rule". It turns "we don't know what we missed" into a number |
| **Learned rule suggestions** | The analyst doesn't need to know blocking. After run 1, PairSight suggests "add *surname sounds alike AND birth year*: it would have caught 18 of 21 confirmed duplicates for 140 extra pairs" |

## What doesn't get better

- **Precision** stays as it is; it's already fair.
- **Jev's accuracy** is unchanged; we're changing what it sees, not how it judges.
- **Cost:** more pairs means more Jev calls and longer runs. The 5,000-pair cap stays the guardrail.

## How we'd know it worked

Build a sandbox file with known hard duplicates planted in it, like the "every vendor missed" cases above. Run it with today's rules and again with derived keys, and check four things:
- how many planted duplicates appear
- how each vendor's recall moves
- whether the "missed by everyone" list fills up
- whether the ranking changes

If none of those move, the feature isn't worth its cost. That's a test we can run before committing to Phase 2 or learned suggestions.

Fuzzy blocking is built and working, and the planted-duplicate check shows a clear gain. The full e2e suite passed 41/41, including a new hard-case test. Nothing is committed.

**What the check measured** (`pnpm blocking:check`: a fictional file of 104 people with 27 planted duplicates; needs no Jev or database):

| Rules | Duplicates found | Pairs Jev would judge |
|---|---|---|
| Default (exact) | 10 of 27 (37%) | 11 |
| Default + fuzzy presets | 23 of 27 (85%) | 24 |

- The plan aimed for 90%; I've set the unit test to the 85% it actually reaches.
- Of the 4 misses, 2 are nicknames (Liz / Elizabeth), deliberately out of reach of every rule.
- The other 2 are real limits of the sounds-like algorithm: Thompson / Thomson keeps the silent P, and Sean / Shaun get different codes. I left them in rather than tune the presets to my own file, and recorded them in V1 Scope.
- I wrote this file myself, so it demonstrates the effect rather than proving it on real data.

**What changed**
- **Fuzzy rule fields:** a rule can now match on how a name sounds (any word order), its first 4 letters, its words in any order, an email username or domain, a birth year, a postcode district, or a phone's last 7 digits.
  - These are stored as plain text like `last_name~sound`, so existing rules and runs are untouched and there's no migration.
  - A field that's too broad alone (birth year, district, email domain, first initial) needs another field beside it. The editor won't save it alone, and the server rejects it too.
- **New presets, off by default:**
  - People: similar-sounding surname + birth year; similar-sounding name + postcode district; same email username.
  - Organisations: name words + district; name start + city.
  - Products: name words + brand.
- **Rules screen:** a **Fuzzy** tag and a one-line note explain these rules, and labels read "Last name (sounds like) AND Date of birth (year)". The custom rule editor has a "Match … on" choice for each field.
- **Results:** the "Findings by candidate source" panel adds a **Hard cases** block: how many duplicates only fuzzy rules found, how many each vendor caught, and a "See these pairs" link. The pairs list gains an "Only fuzzy rules" filter.
- **PDF report:** it carries the same Hard cases line.
- **New dependency:** `double-metaphone` (MIT, no dependencies, server-only) for the sounds-like codes.
- **Docs:** V1 Scope, Product Overview, the roadmap (derived rule fields now marked Built) and the UX plan's "What shipped" are updated.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=142 session=90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137]
timestamp: 2026-10-04T14:15:08.793Z
time: 2026-10-04 14:15
chars: 2546

There's no similarity score involved. It's **blocking on derived keys**: each record gets a looser key computed from a field, and two records land together when their keys are exactly equal. "Fuzzy" comes from the key, not from comparing strings.

**The one real algorithm: Double Metaphone** (Lawrence Philips, 2000), used by "sounds like"
- It turns a word into a code for how it's pronounced in English:
  - Smith and Smyth both give `SM0`.
  - Catherine and Kathryn both give `K0RN`.
  - Jon and John both give `JN`.
- It knows a lot of spelling rules: silent letters, "ph" sounding like "f", "c" sounding like "k" or "s", common Germanic, Slavic and Spanish spellings.
- It returns a primary and an alternate code. I use only the primary, because the blocking engine gives each record one key per rule.
- For multi-word names, I code each word and sort the codes, so "Jon Smyth" and "Smith John" share a key.
- It's the `double-metaphone` package, in `src/server/pairsight/key-transforms.ts`.
- I chose it over Soundex, the older classic. Soundex keeps the first letter as-is, so Catherine and Kathryn never meet, and it handles far fewer spelling rules.

**The other transforms are plain rules, not algorithms**
- **First 4 letters:** letters and digits only, the first four.
- **Words in any order:** split into words, de-duplicate, sort.
- **Email username / domain:** split at `@`; the username drops any `+tag`.
- **Birth year:** the first four-digit year found (19xx or 20xx).
- **Postcode district:** the UK outward code (`SW1A 1AA` → `SW1A`), otherwise the first 3 characters.
- **Phone last 7 digits:** taken from the digits as written, so a number saved without its country code still matches.

**What it isn't**
- **No edit distance or similarity score:** no Levenshtein or Jaro-Winkler, and no threshold like "90% similar". That would be the Phase 2 "similar name" rule from my earlier take, which isn't built. The test-only Jev stub (`scripts/jev-stub.mjs`) uses a one-edit check to fake Jev's scores, but that's not part of blocking.
- **No machine learning.** Every key is deterministic and explainable: two records were compared because their codes matched.

**Limits that come with it**
- Double Metaphone is English-leaning. It misses some spellings: Thompson and Thomson differ because it keeps the silent P, and Sean (`SN`) and Shaun (`XN`) code differently. Those were 2 of the 4 misses in the check.
- No key-based method catches nicknames like Liz / Elizabeth. That would need a nickname table, or the similarity rule.

Before anything new: the fuzzy blocking work is still uncommitted on `feat/fuzzy-blocking-derived-keys`. Since you've tested it, the next step there is `/local-review`, the full e2e, grouped commits and the PR, whenever you say so. The optional sandbox re-record is also still waiting on your yes or no.

After that, the list stands like this:

**On the roadmap, set aside by you earlier**
1. **Human review:** the planned "Next" step. Reviewers correct Jev's call on a pair, so scores can be measured against human labels and not just Jev. Its controls are built but hidden.
2. **Analytics events:** PostHog tracking through the typed event list.

**Follow-ups to what we just shipped** (the check showed a real gain, so these are now justified)
3. **Rule suggestions learned from past runs:** after a run, suggest rules built from the confirmed duplicates (especially the ones only a vendor found), with the evidence shown, e.g. "would have caught 18 of 21 for 140 extra pairs". This is the learned blocking we discussed.
4. **Near-miss sampling:** send Jev a small sample of pairs outside every rule, to report how many duplicates the rules still miss.
5. **A "similar name" rule:** real similarity matching (Jaro-Winkler with a threshold). It would catch what sound codes miss, like Thompson / Thomson. Nicknames would still need a nickname table.

**Housekeeping**
6. **Prompt-audit fixes:** the stale docs and skills from the audit (old admin-layout references, `seed-for-pr` calling a missing `pnpm tsx`, "CI generates migrations" lines, the screenshot step in `/cicd`). The proposed diff exists; it was never applied.

**Later, bigger**
7. A background worker and larger files: the current limits are 500 records and 5,000 pairs per run.
8. Vendor-specific import formats, and legacy `.xls` files.
9. Verified ground-truth uploads, and an "uncertain" result category.
10. The live on-premises deployment, with Jev on the customer's own infrastructure.

My suggestion: ship this branch first, then **rule suggestions (3)**. It builds directly on the derived keys, uses labels every run already produces, and makes fuzzy rules useful without the analyst needing to understand blocking. If you'd rather close the roadmap's main gap, **human review (1)** is the bigger product step.

**Why keep it:** it's the analyst's own word. Switching to something like "matching tool" would make PairSight sound unlike the conversation they're having.

**Where it does confuse:**
1. **On organisation records it collides with the data.** In procurement and ERP systems, the supplier list is literally called the "vendor master". Someone checking that file would see "vendors" as both the companies in their records and the tools being compared. Our own demo shows the problem: "Supplier master — vendor check".
2. **It's never defined.** No screen says what a vendor is in PairSight. The term is assumed from the start.

- Rename the demo evaluations to drop the double meaning, for example "Supplier master — matching check". That changes the recorded demo data and a few e2e names.

The alternative is replacing "vendor" everywhere with "matching tool". I'd advise against it: it's another few hundred strings and test changes, for a word that's clearer to the people actually using the product.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=152 session=90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137]
timestamp: 2026-10-04T21:47:52.531Z
time: 2026-10-04 21:47
chars: 3006

Yes. Waiting for a run isn't necessary. What the rule picker needs is some evidence of which pairs are probably duplicates, and two sources are already there before anything is sent to Jev.

**1. The records themselves: learning without any labels**

There's a classic statistical method for this, the Fellegi–Sunter model fitted with the EM algorithm. It's how record linkage was done long before machine learning. It looks at how often fields agree across all possible pairs:
- Two random records almost never share a phone number.
- Records sharing a phone, a surname and a birth year are far more common than chance would explain.

From those patterns it estimates, with no labels at all, how much each kind of agreement suggests "same". That gives every pair a "probably the same" score.

At our size this is cheap: 500 records is about 125,000 pairs, which takes seconds on the server, with no Jev calls and no cost.

The rule picker then chooses the rules that catch the most likely duplicates for the fewest pairs, the same greedy method as before. It just starts from these estimated duplicates instead of Jev's confirmed ones. It also doesn't use the vendors at all, so it can't tilt the comparison toward any one of them.

**2. The vendor files: a check you already have**

Before any run, we know which pairs each vendor matched. So we can tell you upfront: "these rules would also find 19 of the 21 pairs your vendors matched." That's a recall check on the rules themselves, available on run 1. I'd use vendor pairs only for this check, not to choose rules, so the rules stay neutral.

**3. Then make it zero clicks**

Rather than a "suggestions" panel to review, the Rules page simply arrives already set up for your data:
- "We picked these rules for your data: they should catch about 94% of likely duplicates in 2,100 pairs."
- Each picked rule shows a short reason, and you can change or undo anything.

Someone who never opens Rules still gets well-chosen rules. After a run, Jev's answers can quietly sharpen the estimate for the next one, but nobody has to wait for that.

**Honest limits**
- These are estimates. If a file has very few duplicates, or fields that are mostly empty, the model has little to learn from. In that case it falls back to today's suggested rules and says so.
- Its "probably the same" isn't Jev's judgment. It only decides which pairs get checked; Jev still decides the answers.
- It's built for our current size. A future 50,000-record mode would need a cheaper version.

**Rough shape of the work**
- The scoring model.
- The rule picker, which reuses the derived keys and the planted-duplicate check we already have, to prove it beats the default rules.
- The "picked for your data" display on Rules.
- The vendor-coverage check.

The planted-duplicate file gives us a ready yardstick: today's defaults find 37% and the hand-picked presets 85%. Automatic rules should land at or above that without anyone touching them.

Want me to plan it on a new branch?

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=153 session=90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137]
timestamp: 2026-10-05T10:56:47.828Z
time: 2026-10-05 10:56

Nothing fundamental blocks bigger runs. The 5,000-pair and 500-record limits were v1 safety limits, chosen because of how the app currently runs work, not because Jev or the database can't take more. Here's what actually breaks as numbers grow, from first to last.

**1. Runs are driven by your browser tab (the real blocker)**

There's no background worker. The page keeps asking the server to check the next 10 pairs, so a run only moves while PairSight is open somewhere. Jev takes about 0.3–0.45 s per pair and we send 4 at a time, roughly 10 pairs a second:

| Pairs | Time | Cost (stand-in rate) |
|---|---|---|
| 5,000 | ~8 min | ~$0.10 |
| 50,000 | ~1.5 hours | ~$1 |
| 500,000 | ~14 hours | ~$10 |

Cost isn't the issue. Time is, and nobody will keep a tab open for an hour. The fix is a background worker: the run continues with the browser closed, and you get told when it's done.

**2. Jev's speed limit is unknown**

We've only ever tested 4 calls at once, and TypeSafe doesn't publish a rate limit. If we can safely send 20–50 at once, the times above drop 5–10×. That needs a live test with your key before we promise anything.

**3. Everything loads into memory in one request**

Fine at 5,000 pairs, but it won't hold at 500,000:
- **Uploads:** a 2 MB request limit, the whole file parsed at once, all records inserted together.
- **Results:** every page view loads the entire run (all pairs and records), then filters and pages in memory.
- **Overview, downloads and the PDF:** each built from the whole run in memory.
- **Rules:** every rule change recounts all records live, which is fine to tens of thousands of records and slow beyond.

The fix is ordinary: stream the upload, insert in batches, and have the database do the filtering, paging and counting.

**4. Comparing every record with every other stops scaling**

This matters for the automatic rules we just discussed. At 500 records, scoring all pairs is 125,000 pairs, which takes seconds. At 50,000 records it's 1.25 billion, which isn't possible. The standard answer: score only the pairs that share something loose, like a sound code, a postcode area or an email username. That's millions, not billions, so it scales with the same idea.

**5. The pair cap itself is a guardrail, not a limit**

Its job is to stop a broad rule from quietly turning into a 3-hour, $20 run. Once runs happen in the background, it should become a warning with the time and cost estimate ("about 2 hours, ~$1.50, start anyway?"), not a wall.

**What I'd suggest**

Treat bigger evaluations and automatic rules as one plan, because both need the same foundation: the background worker plus the database doing the heavy lifting. Roughly:
1. Background worker, batched uploads, and Results paged by the database. This removes the 500 / 5,000 walls.
2. Find Jev's real speed limit live and raise the number of calls in flight to match.
3. Automatic rules for the first run, scoring only the loosely matching pairs so they work at any size.
4. Replace the pair cap with a time and cost estimate.

One thing would shape the design: how big are the files you expect people to bring? 10,000 records, 100,000, or a million? That decides whether the current setup (one server, Postgres) is enough or we need something heavier for the large runs.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=154 session=90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137]
timestamp: 2026-10-05T11:02:43.031Z
time: 2026-10-05 11:02
chars: 4626

Both are achievable on the stack we have: one Next.js server, Postgres, plus a background worker. Neither needs heavy big-data tooling. The difference is that 10,000 is an engineering job, while 100,000 also forces a product decision about whether Jev checks every pair.

**The numbers**

| | Today | 10,000 records | 100,000 records |
|---|---|---|---|
| Every possible pair | 125k | 50 million | 5 billion |
| Pairs your rules pick (typically 1–10 per record) | ≤ 5,000 | ~10k–100k | ~100k–1M |
| Vendor-matched pairs (always checked) | small | ~1k–10k | ~10k–100k |
| Jev time at today's 4 calls at once (~10/s) | ~8 min | 20 min – 3 hrs | 3 – 28 hrs |
| Jev time at ~40 calls at once (if allowed) | — | 5 – 40 min | 40 min – 7 hrs |
| Jev cost (stand-in rate) | ~$0.10 | ~$0.20 – $2 | ~$2 – $20 |
| File size | ≤ 1 MB | ~2–5 MB | ~20–50 MB |
| Rows in the database (pairs + checks) | ~10k | ~200k | ~2M |

Cost stays small at both sizes. The things that hurt are time, memory, and a few hard limits.

## 10,000 records: very achievable

None of this is new technology; it's the plumbing we skipped for v1:
1. **Background worker:** runs continue with the browser closed, and you're told when they finish.
2. **Uploads:** read the file as a stream and insert records in batches, instead of one 2 MB request.
3. **Results from the database:** paging, filters and counts done in Postgres, not by loading the whole run on every page view.
4. **Find Jev's real speed limit**, live with your key, and raise the number of calls in flight to match.
5. **Automatic first-run rules**, scoring only pairs that share something loose (sound code, postcode area, email username). That's a few million pairs at most, seconds in the worker.
6. **The pair cap becomes an estimate:** "about 25 minutes, ~$0.60. Start?"

Everything else holds up at this size: blocking, the rules preview, the pair page, the PDF. And all six items are needed for 100k anyway, so none of this work is thrown away.

## 100,000 records: achievable, with one real decision

Everything from 10k, plus these.

**1. Can Jev check every pair? This is the big one.** Up to a million pairs is hours even at good speed, and depends on a rate limit we haven't measured. The alternative is how surveys and audits work: **check a well-chosen sample.**
- To compare vendors you don't need Jev on every pair. Precision comes from a sample of each vendor's merges. Recall comes from a sample of the pairs the rules picked, weighted toward likely duplicates.
- A few thousand checks give numbers like "Correct merges 91% (± 2%)" in minutes, at almost any file size.
- The catch: the numbers become estimates with a margin, and the "every pair" list shows checked pairs plus a count of unchecked ones.

My suggestion: check everything up to a size that fits a reasonable time, and switch to sampling above it, saying so plainly on screen and in the PDF.

**2. Downloads.** A full CSV of a million pairs is ~200 MB, so it has to be streamed, not built in memory. Excel tops out at 1,048,576 rows per sheet, so very large runs would download as CSV only, or as a workbook of just the disagreements.

**3. Excel uploads.** 100,000 rows is fine as CSV. A 50 MB `.xlsx` takes a lot of memory to open, so we'd need a streaming Excel reader, or to recommend CSV for very large files.

**4. Overview charts and the PDF.** These would be computed with database queries rather than in memory.

**5. Bigger groups.** A loose rule can make a single group of thousands of records, which means millions of pairs. Rules would need group-size limits, shown clearly (we already flag big groups).

**6. If Jev runs on-premises, the customer's hardware sets the speed.** Sampling makes that much less of a worry.

## How I'd go step by step

1. **Measure first (small):** a live Jev throughput test at 4, 10, 20 and 40 calls at once, plus a synthetic 10k and 100k file through the current blocking. That gives real numbers instead of my estimates.
2. **Foundation for 10k:** background worker, streamed and batched upload, database-backed Results, the cap turned into an estimate. Raise the limits to 10,000 records.
3. **Automatic first-run rules** on that foundation.
4. **The 100k step:** sampling (if you agree with the approach), streamed downloads, the Excel reader, database-computed charts.

Two questions shape this:
- **At large sizes, is a sampled estimate with a margin acceptable, or must Jev check every pair?** That's the main fork for 100k.
- **Do you want me to run the live Jev speed test now?** It costs a few cents with your key and tells us how far "check everything" can go.

Before the speed test, here are the three answers. One correction to what I said earlier is in the second answer.

## 1. Fewer pairs, same or better accuracy

You're right to push back. Sampling trades accuracy for speed; there's a better route that improves both. The idea is to stop treating comparison rules as the final filter and use a funnel:

**Step 1: cast a wide net (high recall, cheap).** Use loose keys that miss almost nothing: sound codes, postcode area, email username, birth year, name words. On their own they'd produce far too many pairs. That's fine, because nothing here goes to Jev yet.

**Step 2: weigh how much each pair shares (meta-blocking).** A pair that turns up under several keys is much more likely a duplicate than one sharing only a postcode area. For example:
- Same surname sound, same birth year *and* same district: very likely the same.
- Same district only: almost certainly not.

Score each pair by which keys it shares and how rare those values are (sharing "Smith" means less than sharing "Okonkwo"). Then drop the weak ones. In published results this cuts candidate pairs by 90% or more while keeping almost all true duplicates.

**Step 3: score the survivors locally, for free (Fellegi–Sunter).** The unsupervised model from our last discussion compares the actual values field by field and estimates a probability that two records are the same. No Jev calls are involved. Pairs below a low floor are dropped, and only the plausible ones go to Jev.

**Step 4: prove the floor is safe.** Jev checks a small number of the pairs we dropped. If it finds almost no duplicates among them, the funnel is honest, and the report says so ("We checked 200 of the pairs we left out; Jev found 1 duplicate among them"). If it finds many, the floor moves down automatically.

**Why this is both more accurate and more efficient:**
- The wide net in step 1 catches duplicates that today's strict rules miss entirely. That's the planted-duplicate lesson: exact rules found 37%.
- The scoring in steps 2–3 removes the noise those loose keys bring.
- Jev ends up checking fewer pairs, and better ones.

It's also the same machinery as the automatic first-run rules. Users don't pick rules at all; the funnel builds itself from the data. Manual rules can stay as an option for people who want them.

**Vendor-matched pairs are the one exception:** they're always all checked, because that's what the vendors are being measured on.

## 2. "Server-computed APIs" for charts (correction)

The charts and numbers are already computed on the server. Overview, metrics, the PDF and the downloads all run in server code, and the browser only receives the finished numbers.

The real problem is different: to compute them, the server pulls every pair and record of the run out of the database into memory on each page view, then counts in JavaScript. That's fine at 5,000 pairs and breaks at a million.

So the fix is what you're describing, one layer down: let Postgres do the counting and paging (grouped counts, filtered pages, aggregates per vendor), and expose those as small server queries or APIs. The server only ever handles the page of rows on screen and the totals. Agreed, that's how it should be at scale.

## 3. Why Next.js on the server

Yes, it came from the template. This repo started as `8x-web-template` (the AGENTS.md conventions, Prisma, Supabase Auth, the CI gates), and the template is Next.js top to bottom. Nobody chose it specifically for PairSight's backend, and you never asked for it.

**Is it the wrong tool?** For most of PairSight, no: sign-in, uploads, saving settings, pages and downloads are exactly what it's good at. It's weak at two things we now need:
- **Long-running work.** It handles requests: something asks, it answers. A two-hour run doesn't fit that, which is why runs are driven by the browser tab today. On hosting like Vercel, requests are also cut off after a few minutes.
- **Heavy number-crunching.** Scoring millions of pairs on the same process that serves pages would make the app sluggish while it runs.

**Do we need a different stack?** I don't think so. The usual answer is to keep the web app and add a **worker** next to it:
- A separate Node/TypeScript process in the same repo, sharing the same Prisma models and the same matching code (normalisers, rules, Jev client, everything already tested). Nothing gets rewritten.
- It picks jobs from a queue stored in Postgres itself (e.g. `pg-boss` or `graphile-worker`), so there's no new infrastructure. "Start run" adds a job; the worker does it, records progress, and the page just shows that progress.
- Heavy scoring runs in worker threads, so it can use several CPU cores.
- It fits the on-premises plan: one web server, one worker, Postgres and local Jev, as a few containers on the customer's machine.

**When another language would be worth it:**
- **Python** has *Splink*, a mature, well-tested implementation of exactly this Fellegi–Sunter approach, built to handle millions of records quickly. If we someday go to 1M+ records, a small Python scoring service is the strongest option.
- **Go or Rust** would only matter for raw speed at a scale we're not near.

For 10,000–100,000 records, TypeScript in a worker is enough, and keeping one language keeps everything we've tested.

**One project-rule wrinkle:** queue libraries like `pg-boss` create their own database tables at runtime. Your rules say every table comes from a generated migration, with RLS checked in CI. So we'd either pick a queue whose tables we define ourselves, or document an exception. I'd decide that when we plan the worker.

## Next

I'm ready to run the Jev speed test. It sends fictional pairs to the live API at 4, 10, 20 and 40 calls at once and records speed, errors and rate-limit responses. Each call is logged in `ai_usage` like `jev:check`, and the total is a few cents. Shall I go ahead?

I agree, with one adjustment to what "scale forever" means.

**Why Splink is the right call**
- **It's what we were about to build by hand.** Wide-net blocking, Fellegi–Sunter scoring trained with EM, no labels needed, and adjustments for rare versus common values (sharing "Okonkwo" counts for more than "Smith"). It also has the close-match comparisons we hand-rolled (Jaro-Winkler, Levenshtein, Double Metaphone and more), and it reports how many pairs each blocking rule would produce, which is what our Rules screen shows.
- **It scales by swapping the engine, not the code.** It runs on DuckDB on one machine (millions of records, fast) and on Spark or Athena for hundreds of millions. Same model definition either way, so we'd write it once.
- **It's well tested.** It was built by the UK Ministry of Justice for national-scale linking, is widely used, MIT licensed and actively maintained. That's better than our own TypeScript version of the same statistics.
- **It explains itself.** Every scored pair comes with a breakdown of how much each field pushed it towards "same" or "different". That makes good plain-English copy: "picked because the surnames sound alike and the birth dates match".
- **It gives automatic first-run rules for free.** It learns from the records themselves, with no Jev answers and no second run needed.

**What it doesn't solve: the adjustment to "scale forever"**

Splink makes *choosing and scoring pairs* scale. Three other things still need work regardless:
- **Jev is still the slowest part.** Splink can cut the pairs from your rules to a small, sharp set, but every vendor-matched pair is still checked. At 1M records that alone could be 100,000+ pairs, so Jev's speed limit (today's test) still decides how far "check every vendor pair" goes.
- **The web app still loads everything into memory.** Results, the charts and downloads need the database-side counting and paging we discussed. Splink doesn't touch the web app.
- **Runs still need a background worker** to orchestrate: start the scoring, send pairs to Jev, record progress, finish the run.

So the full picture is Splink plus the worker plus database-side Results. Together, those cover 10k, 100k and beyond without another rethink.

**How the pieces would fit**
- **Web app (Next.js, as now):** uploads, settings, pages, results queries.
- **Worker (TypeScript):** runs the job queue and makes the Jev calls. Jev calls stay in TypeScript on purpose, because your AI-usage rule says every AI call goes through `src/server/ai/` and gets costed there.
- **Scoring service (Python + Splink on DuckDB):** takes an evaluation's records and returns scored pairs plus the model's explanation, and nothing else. Keeping that boundary narrow means the rest of the app doesn't care that it's Python.
- **On-prem package:** four containers (web, worker, Splink service, Postgres) plus the customer's local Jev.

**What changes for current features**
- **Today's comparison rules and close-match keys** become Splink blocking rules and comparisons. Users stop having to build rules: the Rules screen shows "here's what we'll check and why" with the option to adjust.
- **The planted-duplicate check (`pnpm blocking:check`)** becomes the yardstick. Splink should beat the 85% of the hand-picked presets with fewer pairs, or we shouldn't ship it.
- **The TypeScript blocking code** stays only as long as something uses it. Ideally it goes once Splink takes over, so there aren't two engines that can disagree.

**The costs, honestly**
- A second language and runtime to maintain, test and ship (Python, and a pinned Splink version).
- Our standards, gates and CI are TypeScript-only today, so the Python service needs its own lint, tests and build added to `local-review` and CI.
- A dependency we don't control. Pinning a version and testing against the planted-duplicate check on every upgrade covers it.

I think those costs are worth it, because the alternative is re-implementing Splink badly in TypeScript and probably replacing it later anyway.

**Order I'd suggest**
1. **Jev speed test** (approved, ready to run).
2. **Splink spike:** run Splink on the planted-duplicate file and a synthetic 100k file, and measure how many duplicates it catches, how many pairs it produces, and how fast. That's evidence before we commit the architecture.
3. **Plan the foundation:** worker plus job queue, database-side Results, and the Splink service boundary.
4. Then build in steps.

## Why DuckDB rather than Postgres?

Splink can run on Postgres. It's one of its supported engines, so it's not impossible. DuckDB is the better fit for the scoring work, for three reasons:

1. **The work is analytics, not app traffic.** Splink's job is a handful of huge queries: join records against each other to make millions of candidate pairs, compute similarity scores on every one, then repeat over all of them for each EM training round. DuckDB is built for exactly that: it reads data by column, works in batches, and uses every CPU core for one query. Postgres is built for the app's kind of work (many small reads and writes, rows at a time) and does far less in parallel within one big query. Same SQL, but on 100k+ records the difference is minutes versus seconds.
2. **It keeps heavy scoring off the app's database.** Running those joins in the Postgres that serves PairSight would slow down everyone using the app while it runs.
3. **The similarity functions are built in.** DuckDB ships Jaro-Winkler, Levenshtein and the other comparisons Splink uses. Postgres needs extensions for some of them, and Splink's Postgres support is less mature.

DuckDB doesn't add a database to run or look after. It's a library inside the Python process, like SQLite: no server, no accounts, nothing to back up. The flow would be:
- **Postgres stays the single source of truth.** Records, runs, Jev's answers and results all live there, as now.
- **For a scoring job,** the Python service reads that evaluation's records from Postgres (DuckDB can read Postgres directly), scores them in memory, and hands back the scored pairs.
- **The scored pairs are saved to Postgres,** and the DuckDB scratch space disappears.

At 10k records, Postgres would do fine. I'd still choose DuckDB, because it's free to add and it's what lets the same code scale later.

## Why a TypeScript worker? Only because of AI tracking?

That's the firmest reason, but not the only one. Three reasons:

1. **AI usage tracking is a hard rule here.** Every Jev call must go through `src/server/ai/` and `trackAiCall`, which writes a priced row to `ai_usage` that MOAD reads across repos. ESLint is what guarantees an untracked call can't ship, and that guard only exists in TypeScript. A Python worker calling Jev would need its own copy of the pricing, the model map and the row format, and nothing would stop the two from drifting apart.
2. **The run engine already exists and is tested, in TypeScript.** That includes:
   - building the Jev question for each record type
   - judgment keys, so earlier answers get reused
   - retries and backoff, and the run's progress states
   - error reasons, and resuming a stopped run
   - the Jev stub the e2e tests run against

   Moving the worker to Python means rewriting all of that, and its tests, for no gain.
3. **One definition of the database.** The schema is defined once, in Prisma, which is TypeScript. A Python worker that writes runs and results would need its own copy of every table it touches (in SQLAlchemy or similar), and two definitions of one database drift apart.

**Could the whole worker be Python?** Yes. It would make the pipeline one process instead of two. But it costs the three things above: rewriting the run engine, duplicating AI tracking (and changing that rule), and keeping a second copy of the schema.

Keeping Python to one narrow job avoids all of it: **records in, scored pairs out.** It doesn't touch runs or call Jev, and it writes nothing except the scored pairs. TypeScript owns everything else. That keeps Python only for the statistics, which is where it's genuinely better.

These are both decisions for the plan rather than now, but they're my recommendations: DuckDB inside a narrow Python scoring service, and a TypeScript worker that orchestrates runs and makes the Jev calls.
