The short answer: it's not about Python versus TypeScript as languages. It's that the hard part of this job is statistics plus large-scale data crunching, and Splink is years of someone else getting that exactly right. Here's what we need, what makes each part hard, and what Splink does about it.

**1. Generating pairs from many loose rules**
- **The task:** take several wide-net rules (same surname sound, same postcode area, same email username…), produce every pair each one finds, remove repeats across rules, and say how many pairs each rule would create before running anything.
- **In TypeScript:** we already do a small version of this (our blocking code). It's fine at 500 records; at a million it's billions of potential pairs and memory blows up.
- **Splink:** turns each rule into an SQL join and lets DuckDB or Spark execute it across all CPU cores, spilling to disk when memory runs out. It also has built-in tools to count pairs per rule and spot rules that create huge groups.

**2. Comparing two records field by field, in shades**
- **The task:** not just "same or different", but graded levels per field: exact match, very close (a typo), somewhat close, different, or missing. "Smith" vs "Smyth" should count for something; "Smith" vs "Jones" for nothing.
- **In TypeScript:** doable. You'd write each comparison and its levels yourself.
- **Splink:** ships a library of ready-made, tested comparisons for names, dates, emails, postcodes and more, built on Jaro-Winkler, Levenshtein, Double Metaphone and so on. Missing values are handled correctly by default, which is easy to get wrong (a missing value must not count as "different").

**3. Learning what each kind of agreement is worth, without any labels (the core)**
- **The task:** work out how much "same surname" says about two records being the same person, compared with "same postcode area", without anyone telling us which pairs are duplicates. This is the Fellegi–Sunter model trained with the EM algorithm.
- **Why it's hard:** this is the part with traps:
  - **Two kinds of probability.** For each agreement level you need two numbers: how often duplicates agree like this, and how often unrelated records agree like this by chance. They're estimated differently: the chance part from random samples of pairs, the duplicate part from EM.
  - **The blocking blind spot (the subtle one).** If you only look at pairs that share a surname, you can't learn what the surname is worth, because every pair agrees on it. Splink trains in several passes using different blocking rules and combines the results so every field gets estimated properly. Get this wrong and the model quietly overrates fields, with no error to tell you.
  - **Numerical care.** Values are multiplied across many fields, so the maths has to work in logarithms or it underflows. EM must converge, handle fields that are mostly empty, and not collapse into nonsense.
- **In TypeScript:** you'd be writing a statistics library from scratch and testing it against published results. It would take months to trust.
- **Splink:** this is exactly what it's for. It's peer-reviewed, used by UK government departments on national-scale data, and has years of these edge cases fixed.

**4. Making rare values count more**
- **The task:** two records sharing "Okonkwo" are far more likely the same person than two sharing "Smith".
- **In TypeScript:** you'd count every value's frequency and fold that into the model correctly.
- **Splink:** term-frequency adjustment is a built-in option on any comparison.

**5. Scoring every pair and explaining the score**
- **The task:** give each candidate pair a probability, and say *why* in a way we can turn into plain English.
- **Splink:** gives every pair a match weight broken down by field (its "waterfall" chart): surname +4, birth date +6, email −2, and so on. That's ready-made material for "picked because the surnames sound alike and the birth dates match".

**6. Checking the model is any good**
- **The task:** know whether it's working: how weights converged, which rules make too many pairs, and later how accurate it is against Jev's answers.
- **Splink:** has diagnostics for all of this, including accuracy charts when you have labelled pairs. Jev's answers after a run are exactly such labels, so we could measure and tune the model with them.

**7. Running fast at any size**
- **The task:** the same code on 10k, 1M or 100M records.
- **In TypeScript:** Node does one thing at a time by default. To scale you'd add worker threads, memory-efficient arrays and batching, essentially building a mini query engine. (Interesting nuance: DuckDB does have a Node.js version. What's missing in TypeScript isn't the engine, it's the statistics layer from points 3–6.)
- **Splink:** writes SQL and hands it to an engine built for this. Bigger data means switching the backend from DuckDB to Spark, not rewriting.

**Why Splink and not other tools**
- **dedupe (Python):** learns from people labelling example pairs. That's exactly the friction you want to avoid.
- **Zingg (Java/Spark):** also leans on labelled training data, and is heavy to run for small files.
- **fastLink (R):** also Fellegi–Sunter, but an R stack for one component is harder to package and scale than Python plus DuckDB.

Splink is the one that needs no labels, scales from a laptop to a cluster, and explains every score. Those three fit PairSight's goals of no friction, any size, and plain-English reasons.

**In one line:** TypeScript could compute all of this, but we'd spend months re-deriving and debugging statistics that Splink has already proven, and the subtle mistakes in point 3 don't crash anything, they just make the results quietly wrong.

Close, but one part would undermine what PairSight is for. Splink should decide **which pairs get checked**, not **whether a pair is a duplicate**.

**Where the idea runs into trouble**

You're picturing Splink as a confirmer: pairs it's confident about skip Jev, and only the uncertain ones go to Jev. Three problems with that:

1. **Splink is a matcher, just like the vendors.** It's an open-source matching engine. Some vendors may even use the same Fellegi–Sunter method inside. If Splink's answers count as correct, we'd be grading vendors against another vendor, and one that happens to work like Splink would look perfect. The whole value of PairSight is an independent judge that all vendors are measured against equally. That has to be Jev, on every pair that counts.
2. **Splink can't read "what counts as the same".** Your definition says relatives sharing an address are different people, or a subsidiary isn't its parent company. Splink only sees that fields agree. It would be very confident a father and son at the same address with the same surname are the same record. Jev reads your definition; Splink can't. The pairs Splink is "sure" about are exactly where these traps hide.
3. **Two judges make the numbers unexplainable.** If some pairs are judged by Splink and some by Jev, "Correct merges 91%" no longer means agreement with Jev; it means agreement with a mix of two things. We couldn't state that plainly in the report.

**The role that works**

- **Vendors** are the engines under test. Deterministic or probabilistic, we don't know or care.
- **Splink** is the pair-finder. It reads all your records and scores every plausible pair: "how likely is this a duplicate".
- **Jev** is the judge. It answers every pair that gets checked, against your definition.

The flow becomes:
1. Records go into Splink, which returns scored pairs.
2. **Pairs to check** = every vendor-matched pair (always; that's what we're grading) + Splink pairs above a low floor (the likely duplicates vendors might have missed).
3. **Jev checks all of them.**
4. **Safety check:** Jev also checks a small sample of pairs below the floor, to prove we didn't drop real duplicates.
5. **Results** compare each vendor with Jev, as now.

**Where the Jev savings actually come from**
- **The big one:** pairs that are obviously different never reach Jev. Today, loose rules send plenty of "same postcode area, nothing else in common" pairs. Splink scores those near zero, so they're dropped. That's most of the volume.
- **Reuse:** answers from earlier runs are never asked again (we already do this).
- **Only at very large scale, as an option:** checking a sample of vendor pairs instead of all of them.

What we don't save on is the pairs Splink thinks are the same. Those are often the most important findings ("the vendor missed this duplicate"), so Jev must confirm them.

**A good use of your instinct**

Splink's opinion can still be useful as information, just not as a verdict:
- Showing "our model's estimate" next to Jev's on a pair helps explain why it was checked.
- A bigger idea: run Splink as a **free baseline vendor**. Its own matches appear in the comparison like any vendor's: "Vendor A beats the free open-source baseline by 12 points; Vendor B doesn't." For someone deciding whether a vendor is worth paying for, that's a powerful line in the report, and it's fair because Jev judges Splink exactly like everyone else.

So: deterministic or probabilistic vendors are what's tested, Splink finds the pairs worth asking about (and can compete as a baseline), and Jev answers.

Yes, your description of today is right. We make our own pairs, add every vendor pair, send them all to Jev, and compute everything from Jev's answers.

And your idea is a real, well-known pattern. The original Fellegi–Sunter theory, which is what Splink implements, has exactly three zones:
- **Above an upper threshold:** automatically a match.
- **Below a lower threshold:** automatically not a match.
- **In between:** sent for "clerical review", a human expert in the original.

In your version Jev is the reviewer for the middle zone. So it's possible, and it would save Jev calls. The question is whether the "certain" zone is certain enough for PairSight in particular. There are three risks:

1. **"Certain" to Splink isn't certain under your definition.** Splink is most confident when lots of fields agree. But a father and son at the same address, with the same surname and phone, agree on almost everything, and your definition says they're different people. Those pairs land in Splink's "certain" zone and would never reach Jev, the one judge that reads your definition.
2. **It can favour some vendors.** If a vendor matches the way Splink does, most of its merges fall in the "certain" zone and get passed without Jev looking. Its wrong merges in that zone are never caught, so it looks better than it is. A vendor with a different method gets every merge checked by Jev. The grading becomes uneven, which is exactly what a comparison tool mustn't be.
3. **The saving is mostly time, not money.** Jev costs about $0.00002 a pair, so even a million pairs is around $20. What we'd save is run time, and the easy "certain" pairs are the cheap ones to check anyway.

**How to get the saving safely: trust, but verify**

Keep your three zones and add a check:
1. Splink scores every pair, ours and the vendors'.
2. **Certain matches** (above the upper threshold) are accepted without Jev, **except a random sample** that Jev checks anyway, say a few hundred.
3. If Jev agrees with nearly all of the sample (for example 99%+), the zone is trusted and the report says so: "3,000 pairs were accepted as matches by our model. Jev checked 300 of them and agreed with 298." If Jev disagrees too often, the threshold moves up automatically and more pairs go to Jev.
4. **Measure that sample per vendor,** so a vendor whose merges are in the "certain" zone is still graded on Jev's actual answers about them. That fixes the unfairness in point 2.
5. **Everything in the middle zone goes to Jev,** ours and the vendors'.
6. **Below the lower threshold, no vendor pair is skipped.** A vendor matched it, so we need Jev's answer to grade the vendor. Only our own low-scoring pairs are dropped, with their own small safety sample.

**When to use it:** only when a run is big enough for time to matter, say tens of thousands of pairs. For a typical run today (a few thousand pairs, a few minutes), sending everything to Jev is simpler, fully exact and costs pennies. So it would switch on automatically above a size, and the report would say plainly which numbers are exact and which rest on a verified sample.

So yes, your understanding is right, and the idea is sound. The one addition is that the "certain" zone has to be checked by Jev on a sample, not just assumed, or the grading quietly stops being fair.

Item 0 is done. Jev turned out not to be a bottleneck at all, and the 10k file showed the hand-picked close-match rules get noisy at scale. Everything is on `chore/scale-measurements`, not committed. The live test cost about 3 cents in total; those calls are logged in `ai_usage` like `jev:check`.

**Jev speed test** (live, `pnpm jev:speed`)

| Calls at once | Pairs per second | Rate-limited or overloaded | Typical latency |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4 (today) | 9.8 | 0 | 335 ms |
| 10 | 25.4 | 0 | 341 ms |
| 20 | 48.4 | 0 | 339 ms |
| 40 | 97.3 | 0 | 336 ms |
| 80 | 162.2 | 0 | 345 ms |
| 120 | 277.1 | 0 | 344 ms |

Speed rises almost in step with calls at once, latency doesn't move, and Jev never pushed back. I stopped at 120 without finding its limit. The slow part is our engine: 4 calls at a time, driven by the browser tab.

**10,000 fictional people** (`pnpm scale:measure`; no Jev, no database)
- **The file hits the upload limit:** 10,000 rows is 1.00 MB, exactly today's limit. 100,000 would be about 10 MB, so large uploads (item 3) are definitely needed.
- **Computing is not the problem yet:** reading took 15 ms and blocking 0.4 s, with memory under 100 MB.
- **Default rules** find 70.6% of the 989 planted duplicates in 1,188 pairs.
- **Adding the close-match presets** raises that to only 76.5%, for 12,888 pairs, over today's 5,000 cap. One preset ("similar-sounding surname and birth year") makes 11,760 pairs on its own, because common surnames like "Smith" share birth years at this size. On the small planted file the same preset raised the share found from 37% to 85%.
- **Jev time** for those 12,888 pairs: 21 minutes today, about 2 minutes at 40 calls at once, and under a minute at 120.
- **Caveat:** the generator's phone numbers come from a small random range, so some phone-rule pairs are chance collisions.

**What this means for the order**
- **Item 1 (the background worker)** is confirmed as the first real step. With it and a higher number of calls in flight, Jev time at 10k drops from minutes to seconds.
- **The hand-picked rules don't hold up at scale**, which is strong evidence for Splink (items 5–6). Sharing "Smith" should count for little and sharing a rare surname for a lot, and that's exactly what its frequency adjustment does.

**Changes on the branch**
- `scripts/jev-client.mts`: the live Jev call code, now shared by `jev:check` and the new speed test. `jev:check`'s output is unchanged.
- `scripts/jev-speed.mts` (`pnpm jev:speed`).
- `scripts/scale/people.mts`: a seeded generator of fictional people with planted duplicates, created on demand, never committed as data.
- `scripts/scale/measure.mts` (`pnpm scale:measure [size]`).
- `docs/PairSight-Scale-Roadmap.md`: item 0's results and the updated open questions.
- The roadmap links in AGENTS.md and the Delivery Plan from before.

Item 1 is built and tested: runs now keep going with no page open, and can be paused and resumed. Nothing is committed yet; it's all on `feat/background-worker` with item 0's work, ready for you to try.

**What changed**
- **Runs work in the background.**
  - A run worker starts with the server and every second picks up any running run. It checks pairs 40 at a time (`RUN_WORKER_CONCURRENCY`, shared fairly when several runs are going) and marks runs complete itself.
  - No queue library or new tables were needed: a running run is the job, and each pair is claimed in the database so nothing gets checked twice.
  - It lives inside the web server, as you chose, so it reuses all the tested engine code.
- **The Runs centre only watches.** It reads each run's progress every second and still takes you to Results when your run finishes. It now also notices a run started in another tab.
- **Pause and Resume.** A running card has a "Pause run" button. Pairs already being checked finish; the card then reads "You paused this run. Resume whenever you’re ready: nothing is lost."
  - I labelled it "Pause run" rather than "Stop", since Resume undoes it. Say if you'd prefer "Stop".
- **Bug fix found while exploring.** Starting a run used to put every earlier failed pair back in the queue, including pairs from older finished runs, which then sat "not checked yet" forever. Now only the new run's own pairs get retried.
- **Two database indexes** for the worker's lookups. The migration was generated with the repo's script and has already been applied to your local database.

**How it was tested**
- **E2E: all 44 pass.** That's the 41 existing tests, now driven by the worker, plus 3 new ones:
  - a run finishes after its tab is closed
  - pause part-way, then resume to completion (the Jev stub gained a "slow" switch for this)
  - a second tab notices a run another tab started
- **Live, one real run:** 500 fictional people and 40 pairs at 40 at once finished in **2.4 s**. All 40 Jev calls were recorded and priced in `ai_usage` (about $0.0009), and the worker marked the run complete.
- **Gates:** lint, typecheck, formatting, env, secrets, AI models, RLS, migration drift and the build pass. Unit tests show 307 passes, including new ones for the concurrency sharing, plus the same 10 Windows-only failures.

**Docs updated:** the Delivery Plan's "Run execution" section, the UX plan's Runs centre, the README, and the roadmap (item 1 marked done, with what's left for later items).

**To try it on your :3000 dev server:** restart it once. The worker starts at server boot, so a hot reload won't start it.

Once you've tested it, the commits would be grouped as: item 0's measurement scripts, the roadmap doc, the worker, the e2e tests, the docs, then the session log.

The test sets are in **Downloads/PairSight - Datasets**, with a README.txt. They're sized to today's limits (500 records and 5,000 pairs per run), so they fill a run without hitting the cap.

| Set | Records | Vendor files ("aggressive" / "careful") | Pairs to check | Run time with the real key, 40 at once | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `people-medium` | 500 | ~940 / ~330 pairs | ~1,230 | ~15 s | ~$0.03 |
| `people-large` | 500 | ~3,240 / ~1,220 pairs | ~3,530 | ~40 s | ~$0.07 |

- The pair count mostly comes from the vendor files, because every pair a vendor matched is always checked.
- The "aggressive" vendor finds almost every planted duplicate plus lots of wrong matches. The "careful" one finds about 70% with fewer wrong matches. That gives Results something real to compare.
- I counted the pairs from the actual files; the run times and costs are estimates from the speed test.

**To watch a job properly:** at the default 40 calls at once, the large set finishes in about 40 seconds. For a slower run, add `RUN_WORKER_CONCURRENCY=10` to `.env.local` and restart the dev server; the large set then takes about 2.5 minutes. That's enough time to pause, close the tab, reopen, check it carried on, and resume.

**Things worth trying:**
- Start a run, close the tab straight away, then come back to the evaluation and see the results waiting.
- Pause part-way: the card says "You paused this run", and Resume carries on from where it stopped.
- Open PairSight in a second tab and open Runs there: it shows the run the first tab started.
- Run the same evaluation again with the same settings: every score is reused, so it finishes instantly and costs nothing.

The files are reproducible with `pnpm scale:datasets "<folder>"`, a small script I added to the branch (`scripts/scale/datasets.mts`). The two older files already in that folder were left alone. Remember to restart your :3000 dev server first, so the worker starts.

Here's everything since the last devlog list, which ended on 3 Oct with the record types and field dictionary committed and not yet pushed.

## 4 Oct: PR #9, record types and field dictionary (merged)
- Committed the session log and pushed `feat/entity-types-and-field-dictionary`.
- Opened PR #9, "Organisations and products, type detection and a bigger field dictionary". Full e2e 40/40, latest `main` included. You merged it.

## 4 Oct: discussions on fuzzy blocking and learned blocking
- **Fuzzy blocking:** concluded it's a fairness issue, not a nice-to-have. Exact rules only find easy duplicates, so every vendor's recall looks better than it is, and hard duplicates no vendor found never show up.
- **Derived rule fields vs. similarity rules:** derived keys (sounds-like, first letters, words in any order, birth year…) are the cheap, explainable form; similarity scoring is the stronger form.
- **Learned blocking**, explained in full: learned *matching* vs. learned *blocking*, rule learning vs. deep-learning blocking. I corrected my earlier claim: we do have labels, because Jev's answers after a run are labels. The real obstacles are needing a first run, only labelling pairs someone already proposed, and very few positives.
- **"What gets better":** honest recall, a "missed by every vendor" list, and the hard-case story that can flip a recommendation.

## 4 Oct: close-match rules (`feat/fuzzy-blocking-derived-keys`)
- **Derived rule fields**, stored as `field~transform`, so no migration was needed:
  - sounds like (Double Metaphone, any word order), first 4 letters, words in any order
  - email username or domain, birth year, postcode district, phone's last 7 digits
  - fields too broad on their own need a partner field
- **New presets per type**, off by default: surname sound + birth year, name sound + district, email username, name words + district, name start + city, name words + brand.
- **Rules screen:** a "Match on" choice per field in your own rules, a Close match tag, and labels like "Last name (sounds like) AND Date of birth (year)".
- **Hard-to-spot duplicates:** shown on Results and in the PDF (found only by close-match rules, confirmed by Jev, caught per vendor), with a filter to list them.
- **Proof** (`pnpm blocking:check`, 104 fictional people, 27 planted duplicates): exact rules find 37%, plus close-match 85%. Misses: nicknames, plus two Double Metaphone limits (Thompson/Thomson, Sean/Shaun).
- New dependency: `double-metaphone`.
- **Fix found along the way:** Docker Desktop wasn't running, so Supabase was down; restarted it.
- Explained the algorithm on request: there's no similarity score, just exact match on derived keys, with Double Metaphone for sounds-like.

## 4–5 Oct: plain-language copy pass (same branch)
- **Two audits:** about 470 of ~1,200 strings needed rewriting. The AI judge had four names, "export" and "reference" each meant two things, there was developer-only text in the UI, and many inputs asked for something without saying why.
- **`docs/rules/copy.md`:** who we write for, the what/why/helps rule, one word per thing (Jev, comparison rules, groups, close match, match cut-off, vendor file, download, record ID), and "agreement, not correctness".
- **Your decisions:**
  - Precision, recall and F1 became Correct merges, Duplicates found and Overall score, with the technical names in hover tips and the PDF.
  - Keep "Jev", always introduced.
  - Groups named by who says what: Both say same, Only Jev says same, Only vendor says same, Both say different, Not checked yet.
- **About 520 strings rewritten.** Developer-only errors rewritten; step pages got tab titles; the sandbox is called "Demo" on screen; metric tips added on the dashboard.
- **`src/i18n/copy.test.mts`:** fails if retired jargon comes back.
- **Conversational voice**, after your feedback ("two normal human beings talking"): headings and prompts became questions ("Which records should we check?", "What's this vendor called?", "How sure must Jev be?", "Ready to start?"), plus a "two people talking" rule in the guide.
- **Your tweaks:** the "Detailed analysis" tab name came back, and "vendor" stays (I explained where it can confuse; you decided to keep it).
- **Landing:** now says plainly that PairSight checks matching vendors and isn't one ("We check matching tools. We don't replace them.").
- E2E updated in about 100 places, 41/41 passing.

## 5 Oct: PR #10 (merged)
- `/local-review`, then full e2e 41/41.
- Six feature commits plus the session log.
- PR #10, "Close-match comparison rules and plain-language copy everywhere". You merged it.

## 5 Oct: discussions on scaling
- **Automatic first-run rules:** you asked for no second-run friction, which leads to unsupervised Fellegi–Sunter scoring.
- **What blocks bigger runs:** browser-driven runs, Jev throughput, whole runs loaded into memory, and all-pairs blow-up. A 10k vs 100k feasibility table.
- **Reducing pairs smartly:** the funnel (wide net → weigh what pairs share → local score → floor → Jev, with a safety sample).
- **Charts:** they were already computed on the server; the real fix is having Postgres do the counting.
- **Why Next.js:** it came from the 8x template. Keep it for the web app and add a worker.
- **Splink adopted for the future:** why Splink over TypeScript, item by item, and why DuckDB rather than Postgres for the scoring.
- **Why the worker is TypeScript:** AI-cost tracking, the tested run engine, one Prisma schema.
- **Splink's role:** it picks which pairs to check; Jev stays the only judge. Your auto-accept idea became the three-zone design with Jev checking a sample per vendor, only for big runs.
- **`docs/PairSight-Scale-Roadmap.md`:** everything decided, in build order (items 0–9), each planned and built on its own. Linked from AGENTS.md and the Delivery Plan.

## 5 Oct: item 0, measurement (`feat/background-worker`, uncommitted)
- **`pnpm jev:speed`** (live, about 5 cents in all): 4 → 120 calls at once, no rate-limit or overload responses, 9.8 → 277 pairs per second, latency flat around 340 ms. Jev isn't the bottleneck.
- **`pnpm scale:measure`** on 10k synthetic people:
  - the file is 1.00 MB, exactly the upload limit
  - computing is fast (reading 15 ms, blocking 0.4 s)
  - default rules find 70.6% in 1,188 pairs; close-match presets 76.5% in 12,888 pairs (one preset makes 11,760)
  - so hand-picked rules get noisy at scale, which supports Splink
- `scripts/jev-client.mts` now shared by both Jev scripts.
- A seeded people generator, after fixing a record-ID bug in it.

## 5 Oct: item 1, background worker (same branch, uncommitted)
- **Your decisions:** the worker runs inside the web server (almost every server module is `server-only`), and a pause control.
- **The worker:**
  - starts at server boot and checks pairs 40 at a time (`RUN_WORKER_CONCURRENCY`), shared fairly across runs
  - finishes runs itself
  - no queue library: the run is the job, pairs are claimed in the database
- **Runs centre** watches instead of drives, and notices runs started in another tab.
- **Pause / Resume.**
- **Fix:** starting a run no longer re-queues older runs' failed pairs.
- **Live check:** 40 real pairs at 40 at once finished in 2.4 s, all recorded and priced.
- **Test sets in Downloads/PairSight - Datasets** (`pnpm scale:datasets`):
  - medium and large, with an aggressive and a careful vendor
  - rebalanced after you spotted the careful vendor wasn't careful
- **Your bug report:** a paused run still showed Results. Results for unfinished runs now show progress, not numbers (pairs aren't checked in any order, so partial scores mislead). Downloads refuse unfinished runs.
- **Bug found by the failing e2e tests:** your dev server's worker took the test server's runs on the shared database. Fixed with worker pools (`RUN_WORKER_POOL`; e2e uses `e2e`).
- **One migration** (`add_run_worker`): the pool column plus two indexes.
- **E2E:** 44/44, including runs finishing with the tab closed, pause → no-numbers Results → resume, and a second tab noticing a run.
- **Pending:** restart your :3000 dev server so its worker gets the pool filter, then I re-run e2e to confirm no real Jev calls go to test runs.

   - If it's **still running:** the left menu says the run is in progress, Results shows "Jev is still checking this run" with how many pairs are done so far, and the Runs button shows it under Active with live progress.

- **Make sure the dev server was fully restarted** after my last changes. Otherwise its worker is still the old version, which works for this test but doesn't have the pool fix.
- **Closing the tab is fine; stopping the dev server isn't.** The worker lives inside the server. If you stop it mid-run, the run waits and carries on when the server starts again; pairs that were being checked at that moment are picked up again after about a minute.

I've built item 2 (Results from the database) and run all the checks. Nothing is committed yet, so you can test it first.

**What changed**
- **Overview:** when a run finishes, the worker works out its Overview once and stores it on the run. Every number comes from that stored summary exactly, with no sampling: the Overview, the cut-off preview, the dashboard and the PDF. The preview no longer gets one entry per pair from the server.
- **Shared checks:** when another run, or "Check this pair again", re-checks a shared pair, the other finished runs' numbers are cleared and worked out again.
- **Detailed analysis:** every pair is still there. Filters, search and sorting now happen in the database a page at a time, and J/K on the pair page still steps through the list.
- **Downloads:** the CSV and Excel files are now plain links that stream, so the browser shows its own progress. Above Excel's 1,048,575-row limit, the Excel option is greyed out and the menu says to download the CSV.

**How I checked it's exact**
- I wrote `pnpm scale:check-results`. It compares the database results with the old pair-by-pair code on every finished run in your local database.
- All 843 runs match exactly, including a 200,000-pair run: every number, every filtered list, the list order and each pair's position.
- The full e2e suite passes (45 tests). One is new: a later run re-checking a shared pair updates an earlier finished run's numbers.

**Speed** (seeded test runs on your machine)

| | 200k pairs | 1M pairs |
|---|---|---|
| Opening the Overview | 16 ms | 14 ms |
| A Detailed analysis page | 0.06–0.4 s | 0.5–6 s |
| Download every pair as CSV | 7 s | 52 s, memory steady at about 400 MB |
| Working out the summary when a run finishes | 13 s | 94 s |

Two fixes got here. The CSV download was 125 s at 200k because every page re-sorted the whole run; it now walks the records in order through an index. The summary build was 49 s at 200k, mostly re-parsing the same records' phone numbers in every pair; it now parses each one once.

**Decisions for you**
1. **1M pages are over target.** At 1M pairs, the slowest Detailed analysis pages take 4–6 s, against an aim of under 3 s. Those numbers are a worst case: local Postgres in Docker runs out of shared memory on these queries at 1M, so I measured with its parallel workers turned off. A real database server doesn't have that limit. The planned fallback is copying each pair's score and status onto its row; I'd re-measure on a proper server before deciding. This is written up in the roadmap.
2. **Raw SQL.** The new queries in `results-query.ts` use `$queryRaw`, which the database rules allow only where Prisma can't express the query, and it must be called out in the PR. Each one has a comment saying why. Are you OK with that?

**Things to know**
- Your local account now owns two test evaluations: "Scale check: 200,000 pairs" and "Scale check: 1,000,000 pairs". Open the 200k one to click through by hand. The 1M one adds over a gigabyte to the local database, so delete it when you're done.
- The demo runs and runs finished before this change work out their summary the first time they're opened.
- **Standards tests:** 10 `check-standards` unit tests fail on this machine. The cause is Windows: the git hooks aren't marked executable, and the check can't launch `eslint` from `node_modules/.bin` (ENOENT). They don't touch any code in this change; I expect them to pass on CI's Linux.
- All other checks pass: lint, typecheck, formatting, env, secrets, AI models, RLS and the production build.

Docs are updated: roadmap item 2 with the speed table, the Delivery Plan's architecture section, and the UX plan's download menu. Tell me when you've tested it and I'll commit in feature groups and open the PR.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=176 session=90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137]
timestamp: 2026-10-06T11:32:41.091Z
time: 2026-10-06 11:32
chars: 2761

I've fixed it, and the fix covers the pattern behind the crash, not just the line in the error. Opening the 1M-pair evaluation no longer crashes Postgres, and every check passes.

**The bug pattern:** the amount of work for each page view grew with the size of the run. Before item 2, a page loaded the whole run into Node. My first version moved that work into Postgres instead of removing it. Every Results request, even the count shown on the tab, joined all of the run's pairs with their Jev checks and both records' tables. Postgres builds those joins in shared memory, and the local database container only has 64 MB of it. A few such requests at once on the 1M run used it up, and the page crashed. A bigger server would only have pushed the failure to a bigger run.

**The fix:**
- **A Results page now reads one table.** It filters, sorts and counts on `run_pairs` only, and looks up records and checks just for the 25 rows it shows.
- **Each pair carries what the page needs.** Its status, score, sort key and search text are copied onto it when the run's summary is built. That happens in the background, a chunk of records at a time, so the Overview and the Detailed analysis always agree. A rebuild only rewrites pairs whose check changed. This adds one generated migration, `run_pairs_results_snapshot`.
- **The rule is written down** in `docs/rules/database.md` under "Bounded queries", so it applies to every agent and reviewer, and I saved it to memory.
- **A guard stops it coming back.** `pnpm scale:check-results` now reads the plan of every query a Results request makes on the three biggest local runs. It fails if the checks or records tables are read any way other than an index lookup. On each run it also confirms it still flags the old crashing query, so it can't quietly stop working.

**Results:**
- The 1M run works with the database's normal settings, and 12 requests at the same time all finished in 3.7 s.
- Every 1M page is now under the 3 s target: the slowest is 1.6 s, where it was up to 6 s before.
- All 860 local runs still match the old pair-by-pair results exactly, and the request plans are bounded on the three biggest runs.
- The full e2e suite passes (45 of 45), and the build, lint, typecheck and formatting are clean.
- Unit tests: 316 of 326 pass. The other 10 are the same `check-standards` tests that fail on this Windows machine; none of them touch this code.

**One cost:** working out the summary for a 1M-pair run now takes 165 s instead of 94 s, because it also copies each pair's check. This runs in the background when a run finishes; the roadmap notes it as an open item.

Nothing is committed yet. Both test evaluations should open now. When you've tried them, tell me and I'll commit and open the PR.

The demo files are ready in **`Downloads/PairSight - Datasets/Demo walkthrough/`**. I checked them against PairSight's own comparison rules, but haven't run them through the app or Jev yet.

**What's in the folder**
- `demo-customers.csv`: 101 fictional customers (the records file).
- `demo-vendor-a.csv`: 13 matched pairs, from a careful vendor.
- `demo-vendor-b.csv`: 21 matched pairs, from a thorough vendor.
- `answer-key.csv`: for you only, not for upload. It lists every planted duplicate and look-alike and which vendor matched it.
- `README.txt`: the setup steps and a suggested walkthrough.

**What's planted**
- **20 people appear twice:**
  - 10 easy: the same email or phone, formatted differently.
  - 6 medium: a nickname with the same phone, or a new email with the same name and postcode.
  - 4 hard: a surname typo, a move and a new email.
- **6 look-alikes who are different people:** 4 parent/child pairs with the same name and postcode, and 2 spouses sharing a landline.

**The story it should tell**, if Jev agrees with the answer key:

| | Duplicates found | Wrong merges | Correct merges | Duplicates found (score) | Overall |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Vendor A | 12 of 20 | 1 | about 92% | about 60% | about 0.73 |
| Vendor B | 18 of 20 | 3 | about 86% | about 90% | about 0.88 |

So Vendor B is the best overall, and Vendor A is the safest.

**A good demo moment:** the default rules find all the easy and medium duplicates and all the look-alikes, but none of the 4 hard ones. Only the close-match rule "Surname sounds alike + birth year" finds them. Vendor B matched 2 of the hard ones itself, so they're checked anyway. Turn that rule on in Rules and run again: the other 2, which neither vendor found, show up. The earlier scores are reused, so the second run is quick and nearly free.

**Setup:** choose the people record type, set the country to United States, use `record_id` as the reference column, and name the vendors "Vendor A" and "Vendor B". The first run sends about 25 pairs to Jev.

Numbers now use the K/M format everywhere: the Results Overview and Detailed analysis, the dashboard, plan, rules, runs, records, vendors and the PDF. The full e2e suite passes (45 of 45), and the build, lint, typecheck and formatting are clean.

**How numbers look**

| Count | Shown as |
|---|---|
| 999 | 999 |
| 1,200 | 1.2K (your "1200" becomes this) |
| 1,234 | 1.23K |
| 12,345 | 12.3K |
| 1,234,567 | 1.23M |

I kept three significant figures rather than one decimal, so a small run loses as little detail as possible. Hovering a shortened number shows the exact figure, both for numbers on their own (tiles, the matrix, tables, the plan ledger, chart tooltips) and for headline sentences like "1.2K pairs", "Showing 1–25 of 12.3K" and "pair 1.23K of 12.3K". The chart axis uses the same format.

These stay as they were, because they aren't counts: percentages (80.0%), scores and cut-offs (0.62), money, dates, page numbers and run numbers.

**Things to know**
- **The PDF uses K/M too**, without the exact figure, since a PDF can't show a hover. Tell me if you'd rather the report keep full numbers.
- **Plural wording still follows the real number,** so "1 pair" and "2 pairs" stay correct.
- The landing page's sample run now reads "1.2K pairs" and "1.17K already checked" to match.
- One e2e test looked for "5,460 pairs", so I updated it to "5.46K".

**So it can't come back:** there is one shared format (`src/i18n/formats.ts`) and one component (`<Count>`), with two guards:
- **Messages:** a new test, `src/i18n/counts.test.mts`, fails on any count in `en.json` that doesn't use the format. Run against the old messages, it flags 66 places.
- **Components:** lint now fails on the two ways to print a raw count, `format.number(n)` without a named format and `toLocaleString()`.

The rule is also written in `docs/rules/copy.md`.

I'm on `main` with your merge of PR #12 pulled; the only local change is the session log, which the logging hook keeps updating.

**Next on the roadmap is item 3: large uploads.**

**Today's limits:**
- **500 records** per file.
- **1 MB** per uploaded file.
- **One 2 MB server action:** the whole file goes up and is checked in a single request.

Item 2 means Results are no longer what holds bigger files back; the upload itself is.

**What item 3 covers**, per the roadmap:
- **Streaming uploads:** read the file as it arrives and save records in batches, instead of one big request.
- **Big Excel files:** a streaming Excel reader, or a clear "use CSV for very large files" message.
- **Validation:** still reports problems by row number, so people can find them in their file.
- **The record limit:** raised to 10,000 first, the size we agreed to test at.

**Decisions the plan will need from you:**
1. **How files get to the server.** Streaming them to our own API route is simplest locally and on-prem. Uploading to storage first and processing it in the background is the alternative.
2. **Excel above 10,000 rows.** Read it with a streaming reader, or ask for CSV past a certain size.
3. **The 5,000-pair cap per run.** It's a separate item (8, an estimate instead of a hard cap). With 10,000 records the default rules stay under it, but close-match rules may go over, as the 10k measurement showed.

As before, I'll create the branch from `main`, and then you can pass me plan mode to plan item 3.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=183 session=90a4606b-2381-42a5-9032-5e4e39778137]
timestamp: 2026-10-06T13:43:52.646Z

Item 3 (large uploads) is built on `feat/large-uploads`: files of up to 10,000 records and 10 MB now upload, check and save, in CSV or Excel. All checks pass, including the full e2e suite (46 of 46) and a production build. Nothing is committed yet.

**What changed**
- **Upload once.** A file goes up once, with a progress percentage, and is kept on the server while you check it. Picking a sheet or changing a vendor's columns no longer sends the file again. Uploads nobody saves are cleared after a day.
- **Read as a stream.** CSV and Excel files are read a batch of rows at a time, so a big file doesn't have to fit in memory. Problems are still reported with row numbers.
- **Excel reading.** The streaming Excel reader crashes on workbooks that store their sheet list after the sheets, which includes files ExcelJS writes, like our test fixtures. I work around it by reordering the file's contents before reading, without unpacking anything, rather than writing my own Excel parser.
- **Checking columns.** Checking every column as a possible record ID is now one pass over the file. It used to grow with columns × rows × columns.
- **Saving.** Records are saved 5,000 at a time, and a half-saved file is never treated as current. A save that fails part-way leaves nothing behind. Confirming a vendor file also saves in batches, all or nothing.
- **Limits:** 10,000 records, 10 MB per file, and up to 100,000 rows in a vendor file. The cap of 5,000 pairs per run is unchanged; that's item 8.
- **Rules screen:** it now reads the records once per page view instead of twice, and three slow spots in the comparison-rule code are fixed.

**Speed at 10,000 records** (`pnpm scale:measure-uploads`):

| | CSV (1 MB) | Excel (0.7 MB) |
|---|---|---|
| Upload | 0.2 s | 0.1 s |
| Preview, every column checked | 0.1 s | 0.7 s |
| Save | 2.8 s | 3.8 s |
| Rules page | 0.9 s | 0.5 s |
| Plan page | 0.3 s | 0.2 s |

Checking and confirming the vendor file took under 0.2 s each.

**What I found at 100,000 records** (above the new limit, as a check): the upload, the check and the save keep up (0.7 s, 0.8 s and 15 s). The Rules and Plan pages don't: they rebuild every pair on each page view, which takes 10.7 s and 3.5 s. Going past 10K needs that work done once and stored (roadmap items 6 and 8), so I recorded it in the roadmap rather than raising the limit further.

**New tests:**
- **E2E:** a file over 10 MB gets a plain message, and a 10,000-record CSV uploads once, is checked and saved. The Excel test now also checks that picking a sheet doesn't upload the file again.
- **Unit:** checking rows in batches gives the same results as checking the whole file, the true row count is reported past the limit, a 5,000-row workbook reads in batches, and the reordered Excel file still opens normally.

**Ready for you to test:**
- **Test files:** `Downloads/PairSight - Datasets` now has a 10K set: `people-10k-records.csv` and `.xlsx`, plus a careful and an aggressive vendor file.
- **Clean-up:** the timing script left three "Scale check" evaluations in your account (10K CSV, 10K Excel and a 100,000-record one). You can delete them.

When you've tried it, tell me and I'll commit and open the PR.

I've made all the layout fixes and checked them in screenshots at 1600 and 1280 widths. The full e2e suite passes (46 of 46).

**Overview**
- **"Why was each pair checked?"** no longer stretches its row. It takes the height of the cards beside it and its list scrolls inside, with a fade at the edge when there's more below.

  - The "See these pairs" link sits as one line under the list, always visible. It now reads "See the N pairs only close-match rules picked", because it opens every pair those rules picked, not only the confirmed duplicates.
- **The group tiles** stay three across until there's room for five, so names like "Only vendor says same" fit on one line.

**Evaluations page**
- The cards are back to their old sizes, with no scroll box.
- "Pick up where you left off" shows the 3 most recently created evaluations. The number in the corner still counts all of them.
- The latest-results table now fits its card at 1280:
  - The column header is shortened from "Disagreements with Jev" to "Disagreements".
  - The "possibly missed / possibly wrong merges" split moved to the hover on the count, with exact numbers.
- In the evaluations table, a long name ends in "…" with the full name on hover. Last run shows the day, with the time on hover.

**Wrapping across the app.** Short text in tight spots now stays on one line, and anything cut off ends in "…" with the full text on hover:
- every table's header row;
- the card titles, through the shared panel;
- the rule names and the "What's left to look at?" lines on the Overview;
- the vendor stat labels;
- the field checkboxes on Rules, which now use as many columns as fit;
- the field names in the field-patterns table;
- the group names on the Overview tiles.

What I left wrapping:
- **Explanatory sentences**, such as the text under the big numbers, since cutting them off would hide what they say.
- **Record values on the pair page**, because they're the data you compare, and cutting off a long email could hide the very difference you're checking.

**Large uploads (from earlier):** to stop one value repeated on thousands of rows from flooding the ID check, a repeated ID now lists its first 20 rows and "and N more". The problems panel and the sheet picker also scroll inside a fixed height.

I saved these layout preferences to memory so I follow them from now on.

I fixed the timer that kept running after a run finished. The full e2e suite passes (46 of 46), but I haven't watched the new in-between state in a browser yet; it's only on screen for a few seconds.

**What was wrong:** since the last item, a run's results summary is built before the run is marked complete. On your 10K file that build takes several seconds. During that time every pair was already checked, but the run had no finish time yet, so the timer kept counting. The final 0:16 also included the summary build, not just checking.

**What changed:**
- **Finish time:** the moment the last pair is checked, the run records it. The summary is then built, and only after that is the run marked complete. Elapsed time now means checking time only.
- **Runs centre:** once checking is done, the timer stops straight away. The card says "All pairs checked · working out the results" until the results are ready, then moves to Complete and takes you to the results as before.
- **Resume:** resuming a paused run now clears the old finish time, so the timer counts again. Retry failed already did this.

You should see the new label for a few seconds on your next 10K run.

**A follow-up I noticed:** the Runs centre checks a running run's progress every second with a query that joins all of the run's pairs to their Jev checks. That's the same whole-run pattern the "Bounded queries" rule now forbids for Results. It's fine at today's 5,000-pair cap but would get heavy on very large runs. It isn't part of this item, so I'd note it for item 8, when the pair cap goes, unless you want it fixed now.

Unit tests are unchanged: the same 10 Windows-only `check-standards` failures as before. Nothing is committed yet; tell me when you'd like the PR.
